# Changelog

All notable changes to `core-agent` are recorded here. The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/) and the project follows [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## Stability promise

The public API of `core-agent` is the exported surface of these packages:

- `agent`, `eventlog`, `tools`, `permissions`, `config`, `models` (+ `models/anthropic`, `models/gemini`, `models/mock`), `pricing`, `recording`, `runner`, `transcript` (formerly `session`; the old path forwards, deprecated), `usage`, `instruction`, `mcp`, `skills`, `telemetry`

Pre-1.0, breaking changes are possible at any minor version (`v0.X`). When we make one, the change is called out in this file under **Changed** or **Removed**, and non-trivial removals get a one-version deprecation period when feasible. Patch versions (`v0.X.Y`) are bug fixes only.

The `extras/` adapters (`extras/scion-agent/`, `extras/ax-agent/`) and the `internal/` packages they ship with track `core-agent`'s minor version but do not promise their own stability — adapters target moving runtimes (Scion, AX) and follow whatever those upstream projects do.

---

## [Unreleased]

### Breaking Changes

- **`attach.SessionACLStore` gains a `SetTitle` method.** Adding a method to an exported interface breaks any out-of-tree implementation, and there is no way to add one that doesn't — an optional-capability type assertion would have meant the store silently dropping titles on a wrapper that predates the method, which is the failure mode where a feature looks broken and nothing says why. `pkg/attach` is not in the stability promise above, every in-tree implementation (the GORM store and the test fakes) is updated, and the fix for a downstream implementation is one method that writes a column. Related: `SessionACLStore.Put` now treats an empty `Title` as "leave the stored one alone" rather than "clear it" — see the feature entry for why a whole-row upsert had to stop being one. ([#808](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/808))

- **`attach.Entry.ACL` is unexported; read it through the new `Entry.CurrentACL()`.** The field was a plain exported `auth.SessionACL`, written once at registration and never again — safe as a lock-free read only because nothing ever wrote it. `PATCH /sessions/{sid}/acl` (see the feature entry) makes it mutable on a live session while every in-flight request is reading it to authorize itself, so it is now an `atomic.Pointer[auth.SessionACL]` behind an accessor, matching the `lastTouchedNs atomic.Int64` idiom already on the same struct and keeping the authorization path lock-free. Leaving the field exported was the alternative and was rejected in both available forms: guarded-but-exported is a footgun that `-race` catches only if a test happens to exercise the collision, and exported-as-a-registration-time-snapshot means a caller reading `entry.ACL` gets *stale authorization data* with no indication of it. A compile error is the better migration signal for a field that governs who may reach a session, and it is a one-word fix at the call site. Every other mutable field on `Entry` was already unexported behind an accessor; `ACL` was the odd one out only because it had been immutable. `pkg/attach` is not in the stability promise above, and all in-tree readers are updated. ([#797](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/797))

- **`permissions.mode: "allow"` configs that spawn subagents need a new allowlist entry.** `spawn_agent` / `spawn_remote_agent` are gated as of this release (see the `#758` entry under Bug or Regression), and `allow` mode denies anything the allowlist doesn't name — so a headless config that has been fanning out subagents will start refusing them. Add `"spawn_agent:*"` to restore the previous behaviour, or a narrower `"spawn_agent:<subagent-name>"` per delegate. `ask` mode gains a prompt rather than a denial (or, with no prompter wired, the usual `ErrNoPrompter`); `yolo` is unaffected, except that `plan_mode: "required"` now really does hold spawns until a plan is recorded — which is what it always claimed. ([#758](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/758))

### Changes by Kind

#### Bug or Regression

- **`examples/parallel-spawn` had stopped running: all three of its spawns were refused and the example exited 1.** Its children are *ad-hoc* — the parent's model authors each `system_prompt` at spawn time — and ad-hoc spawns became opt-in when the declarative roster landed, on the reasoning that an unattended daemon should only spawn operator-vetted specs. The example never opted in, so every `spawn_agent` came back `background: ad-hoc subagents are disabled` and the run failed on its own "expected 3 spawned subagents, got 0" check, while its README went on claiming "Exits 0". It now passes `background.WithAllowAdhoc(true)` with a comment saying why the default is the other way and what a daemon does instead (fan out by name against a configured subagent). Nothing in CI runs the Go examples, which is how this went unnoticed; that gap is filed separately ([#852](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/852)). The other twelve credential-free examples were smoke-run at the same time and all still pass. ([#476](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/476))

- **A subagent reached synchronously spent money that landed in no ledger at all — not the parent's, not its own — so the session cost ceiling could not see it.** Two doors reach the same declarative subagent. The asynchronous one rolls a spawned run into `parent.Tracker()` ([`background/spawn.go`](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/blob/main/pkg/agent/background/spawn.go)); the synchronous one — a subagent installed as a named tool by `agent.WithSubagents` — did not. (`spawn_agent { wait: true }` is not this door: it blocks on a background run, which was already billed.) In library mode `*Agent.Run` never appends usage itself; the harness draining its event iterator does (`runner.Headless`, `runner.WakeLoop`). A subagent invoked as a tool runs on its **own** ADK runner inside the tool handler, so its events never reach that iterator, and `assembleSubagent` wires a model, a persona and a tool surface but no tracker — so `Inner.Tracker()` is nil in every in-tree deployment. Measured on pre-fix code, a child burning 1000 in / 200 out left the parent's tracker at `in=0 out=0 turns=0 cost=0`. This is worse than the mis-attribution [#713](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/713) describes: `--max-session-cost-usd` is the only real backstop an unattended run has, and it was blind to a whole class of spend — a subagent that wandered for twenty turns moved the session total by exactly zero. `SubagentOptions` gains `ParentTracker`, filled in by `WithSubagents` from the parent's `WithUsageTracker`, and each delegated model turn is appended as it completes. The roll-up goes through `usage.TurnTap` rather than appending per usage-bearing event, because Gemini reports `UsageMetadata` cumulatively across the chunks of one turn and a naive append would both sum running totals and inflate the turn count ([#353](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/353)). Turns are priced and attributed under the **subagent's** model, not the parent's, since a declarative subagent routinely runs a cheaper tier and filing it under the parent's model would misprice the row and misreport the mix. Two consequences are intended rather than incidental: appends fire mid-parent-turn, so an attached operator now sees `usage-update` frames during a delegation (the asynchronous door already behaved this way), and spend that was always real but never billed now counts toward `--max-turn-cost-usd` and `--max-session-cost-usd` — a deployment sitting just under a ceiling on unbilled delegation can begin to trip it. A host that wired no tracker anywhere is unaffected, and lazy resume still replays the parent's row only, so delegated spend is not restored into a rebuilt tracker — the same limitation `spawn_agent` has always had. (Advances [#713](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/713))

- **A skill's opening step could override the goal an agent was delegated, not just its persona — so a subagent asked to diagnose one workload audited a different cluster instead.** Skills load at the point of use, so they speak *last*: after the system instruction, after `AGENTS.md`, and after the delegated goal. The ADK skill toolset's own system instruction tells the model to "follow them exactly as documented" and to complete every step in order, which is right for a procedure and wrong for a skill that opens by re-deriving its task. In live GKE UAT a subagent handed a fully specified goal — one named workload, in one named namespace, on one named cluster — loaded a skill whose Step 0 was "acquire the following context from the user or active `SETTINGS.md` config" followed by a `gcloud container clusters get-credentials` it had no shell to run. With no operator to ask and no `SETTINGS.md`, it improvised against its GKE MCP the only way that goes: it enumerated clusters, audited a different one, and never touched the workload. 44 turns and $1.33 against $0.26 for the comparable run; the parent redid the diagnosis itself, so the answer was right and the failure was visible only in the bill. The subagent's own content root said "**One cluster.** Stay scoped to the cluster you were asked about" and lost too — which is why this is not the persona-layer problem [#703](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/703) closed. Every instruction body served by `load_skill` now ends with `skills.InstructionFraming`: a skill governs *how*, never *what* or *which subject*; parameters the task already supplied are not to be re-acquired; and a step naming a tool the agent does not have is to be skipped rather than improvised around. It is appended after the skill's own text, because recency is the entire mechanism of the bug and a preamble would be read before the Step 0 it exists to overrule. Frontmatter and `references/` files are untouched, and the framing survives `subagents[].skills` scoping — the reported failure was a subagent. ([#711](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/711))

- **A digest subagent's cost was re-derived from raw token counts, so every input token it read from cache was billed as fresh — and the session cost ceiling moved with it.** The `savings` sidecar riding each wrapped tool response carried `subagent_input_tokens` / `subagent_output_tokens` and nothing else, so `extractSavingsRecord` and `chargeDigestSubagent` priced the digest with `Pricing.CostUSD(in, out)`: one flat input rate over the whole prompt. That is the one thing `CostUSD` is documented not to be used for once caching is on — [#263](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/263) split input into three buckets precisely because they are priced differently, and on Anthropic a cache read is a tenth of the base rate while a cache write is a quarter more. With [#714](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/714) turning prompt caching on by default the error is not a rounding one: a digest whose prompt is mostly a cache read overcharges by up to ~10× the read fraction, and one that writes the cache **under**charges — the worse direction, because `--max-session-cost-usd` reads `Totals().CostUSD`, and a ceiling that reads low lets a runaway run further before it trips. The two buckets now travel the whole way: `agent.SubtaskResult` accumulates `CachedInputTokens` / `CacheCreationInputTokens` across the subtask's turns (clamped, so a provider quirk can't price a negative remainder), `pkg/compose`'s LLM-fallback closure forwards them into `mcp.LLMFallbackResult`, `pkg/mcp` writes them onto the sidecar, and `usage.DigestSavingsRecord.SubagentTurn()` reassembles the turn the far side prices with `CostUSDForTurn` — the same single definition of turn cost the rest of the ledger uses. **The cost figure itself deliberately does not cross the sidecar**, which is where this departs from the issue's proposed fix: `pkg/digest.Savings` states that dollar figures are not stored there, so that a historical digest re-prices when rates change, and carrying `SubtaskResult.CostUSD` through would have quietly retired that. Carrying the inputs a cost function needs keeps both properties. The buckets are written **even when zero** rather than omitted, so a recording reader can tell "this producer reports buckets and there was no cache activity" from "this producer is older than the buckets"; a sidecar with no bucket keys prices exactly as it did before, which is correct — a turn with no cache activity has none — and that is still the path every in-tree caller takes until an operator opts into `--mcp-agentic-wrap-llm`. ([#771](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/771))

- **A subagent that stopped talking without returning a result came back to its parent as `stop_reason: natural`, indistinguishable from one that deliberately handed something back.** `autonomous.StopReasonCompleted` is set on two different endings: the done/return tool firing, and `WithStopOnNaturalEnd` observing that the model stopped asking for tools — and in the second case `DoneDetail` is filled from whatever text the turn happened to end on, so the two produce the same-shaped result. In the GKE session that filed this, a cluster-triage subagent ended with "let me know if you would like me to continue" — there was no operator on that loop, and nothing was ever going to answer — and the parent, reading `natural`, built on the non-answer as the report, then re-derived the whole investigation itself after already paying $1.33 for the delegation. `autonomous.RunResult` now carries `Returned`, true only on the return-tool path, and `spawn_agent` reports the two endings as separate classes: `natural` when something was handed back, the new `no_return` when the loop merely ended. Every class but `natural` also gains a `guidance` line — one sentence of plain language telling the parent what the outcome means for its next move, because the parent is a language model and an enum two fields down is exactly what already failed to steer it; on `no_return` it says to check the text against the goal before building on it, and to re-ask or do the work directly. The subagent's text is still handed over in full: turning a non-terminal outcome into a tool error was #691's mistake, where discarding the partial made the parent pay twice for the same diagnosis, and auto-nudging the subagent would re-introduce the blind `"continue"` re-drive that #730 removed from bounded delegation on purpose. Two #730 acceptance tests that asserted `natural` for loops which never called the return tool now assert `no_return`; the one whose fixture calls `mark_task_done` is unchanged. ([#710](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/710))

- **A wake-driven turn showed the model a bare list of queued messages, so two corroborating alerts about a resolved incident read as two new asks and drove a 22-call tool loop.** Two inbox formatters had been drifting since #144: the TUI's auto-continue flow gained explicit branch guidance (treat variants as one ask; after a completed task, respond once), and the daemon's `POST /inject` path — the one every machine signal arrives on — kept the bare `[Inbox]` list. In the GKE session that filed this, `mark_task_done` had already fired when lookout injected two `family.member` blast-radius joins two minutes apart, each of which said in plain text that it was the same incident from a different altitude. There is now one guidance block, shared by both surfaces so they cannot drift again, with a new branch for the shape a watcher actually produces: *corroborating detail on something you already handled → acknowledge it and move on; do not re-open the work*. It is applied when the queued bundle IS the turn — a `Run(ctx, "")` from `runner.WakeLoop`, the REPL's wake path, the daemon after an inject — and deliberately withheld when the operator typed a prompt of their own, where "respond once to the bundle" would compete with the request sitting right below the separator. That question is asked of the operator's raw text, not of the prompt argument, so a turn that happens to carry a background-agent alert still counts as nobody-asked. The wording moved off "notes" and "operator" and onto "messages", since the surface that needed this most is machine signals, which are neither. The `[Inbox]` header is unchanged, and an empty turn no longer ends in a dangling `---`. ([#697](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/697))

- **Every Anthropic turn reached `pkg/usage` unattributed, so a resumed session priced all of them against `defaultModel` — including the cheap subagent tiers.** `pkg/models/anthropic` never set `LLMResponse.ModelVersion`, and `RebuildTrackerFromEvents` reads exactly that field, falling back to `defaultModel` when it's empty. The fallback's comment says the session's primary model is stable across its lifetime so this is fine, which is true of a single-model session and false of every multi-tier one — and with this provider the fallback was not a rare path, it was the only path. Two consequences, the second being the one that bites: cost is wrong in whichever direction the tiers differ (subagent turns billed at the main model's rate overcharge, the reverse undercharges), and **a tier assignment cannot be checked** — verifying that the cheap tier really runs the cheap model is the main reason to have tiers, and one undifferentiated total is precisely the record that can't answer it. Downstream, a per-agent cost breakdown isn't derivable at all, since an agent's tokens are only priceable once you know which model ran them. The terminal response now carries the model that served it, taken from the accumulated `anthropic.Message` the adapter already holds — no extra call, no plumbing. **The server's echo wins over the model we asked for**, because an alias resolves server-side to a dated snapshot (`claude-sonnet-4-5-latest` → `claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929`) and the snapshot is the billed one; pricing an alias-requested turn against the alias is the same class of mistake as pricing it against `defaultModel`. Two echoes are rejected in favour of the requested ID, both because what this field feeds is a pricing key: an empty one (impossible on a well-formed `Message`, since `model` is required on the API response, but reachable on a stream that died before `message_start`), and a resource path — checked against the real catalog rather than assumed, since `pricing.Catalog` resolves `claude-sonnet-4-5-20250929` and the Vertex `claude-opus-4-5@20251101` shape through its longest-prefix fallback but matches nothing under a `projects/…/models/claude-opus-4-5`, which would price the turn at $0. The streaming partials are deliberately left unstamped: they carry no `UsageMetadata`, so nothing prices them, and a test pins that. A live check would have been weaker than it looks — an alias and its snapshot usually agree, so a test that only requests a concrete model passes whether or not the echo path works; the offline SSE double makes them differ on purpose. ([#756](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/756))
- **An interrupted turn was reported to attached clients as a retryable transient network error. The attach protocol goes 1.7.0 → 1.8.0 with a new `canceled` turn-error kind.** `ClassifyTurnError` mapped `context.Canceled` to `kind: "transient_network"`, `code: "CANCELED"`, `retryable: true` — a payload that contradicts itself, and worse, answers the one question the protocol asks a client to decide from it with the exact opposite of what the operator asked for. `retryable` exists so a client can offer "try again"; an interrupt is the one case where trying again re-runs work someone deliberately stopped. No consumer branches on the flag today — `pkg/tools/peer` renders kind + message, `internal/coretuiremote` passes it straight through, and core-tui v0.22.0 spends it on one `↻ retryable` line — so nobody has been bitten yet — but [core-tui#260](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/260)'s ESC cascade dispatches down this path, and [#803](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/803) has just made `RemoteInterrupter` reachable from the local host too, so the shape had to be right before a client keys off it. The telemetry half is already live rather than latent: `recordInvocation` stamps the same classifier kind as `error.type` on `gen_ai.agent.invocation.duration`, so wherever metrics are enabled every operator interrupt has been counting as a transient network failure on any dashboard or alert keyed to that rate. `canceled` carries `retryable: false` and the message loses its "model call" framing — the cancel is not necessarily observed at the model call, and for an interrupt the accurate statement is that the turn was stopped. **The deadline branch immediately above is deliberately untouched**: `context.DeadlineExceeded` stays `transient_network` / retryable, because a call that ran out of time genuinely is worth retrying, and a test asserts that boundary so a later "make context errors non-retryable" simplification can't quietly take it with it. One classification serves every producer, so no cancel-reason plumbing is needed: an operator `/interrupt`, a parent-context cancel at shutdown, and a guardrail cutting a turn short are all deliberate stops. That third producer is worth stating because the issue's scoping missed it — `cost_ceiling` and `watchdog` do construct their own `TurnError` directly rather than through the classifier, but their **in-flight** arms (`enforceCostCeilingInTurn`, `enforceWatchdogInTurn`) halt by calling `Interrupt()`, so the cut turn's own error comes back cancelled and reaches the classifier anyway. That pairing is unchanged and predates this fix — an in-flight halt emits the guardrail's frame first (carrying the reason and the reset affordance) and the cancel frame second; only the second one's `kind` moves, from a wrong `transient_network` to a right `canceled`. Additive minor, exactly as `cost_ceiling` and `watchdog` were: no new event type, so `supportedEventTypes` is deliberately unchanged and a test pins that it stays that way. Checked against the pinned client rather than assumed — core-tui v0.22.0 maps only an *empty* kind to `unknown` (it doesn't even define the `cost_ceiling` / `watchdog` constants, let alone switch on them), prints whatever string it was handed into the error row's header, and gates its retry line on the boolean — so the new value renders correctly with no pin bump and the client half can follow at its own pace. ([#816](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/816))
- **A turn refused by a tripped guardrail recorded `error.type: unknown`, so the spend-cap and runaway series went dark during exactly the incidents they exist for. `pkg/attach` gains `SelfClassifyingError`.** `recordInvocation` stamps `attach.ClassifyTurnError(err).Kind` onto `gen_ai.agent.invocation.duration`, and the classifier is substring-based by design — it matches gRPC status names and HTTP codes so that a change of wrapper layer upstream can't silently regress a type switch. A guardrail refusal is not from upstream: `costCeilingError` and `watchdogError` carry the operator-facing reason as their whole message ("per-turn cost ceiling exceeded: this turn cost $0.1500…", "watchdog halted the agent (repeated-tool-call): …"), which matches none of the classifier's patterns, so both fell through to `unknown`. `cost_ceiling` and `watchdog` were therefore the only two kinds in the enum that **no classifier path could produce**, even though both are documented in the kind table and both are emitted on the wire — by the guardrails themselves, directly, which is why the wire was right and the metric was wrong. The watchdog case was worse than the ceiling's rather than merely equal to it: its reason embeds arbitrary trigger prose, so which branch a refusal landed in depended on what the runaway happened to be looping on. The repeated-tool-call alert formats the offending tool's **name and the first 200 bytes of its JSON arguments** into the reason, and the cycle detector formats the whole call sequence in, so the substring scan was reading model-supplied text: a tool named `parse_manifest` classified the halt as `config_error`, and args carrying the words `not found` classified it as `model_not_found`. `unknown` was merely the common case, not the behaviour. Fixed by inverting the dependency rather than by adding needles: `pkg/agent` imports `pkg/attach` to emit events, so `pkg/attach` cannot import it back and a type switch there is impossible, but an interface can go the other way. `SelfClassifyingError` lets an error declare the payload it should be reported as, `ClassifyTurnError` consults it via `errors.As` ahead of every heuristic including the context checks, and each guardrail error returns the same payload its own trip emit puts on the wire — one construction site feeds both, and a test asserts the two agree rather than a comment claiming they do. **Nothing changes on the wire.** A turn refused at the top emits no frame at all today (it points back at the `cost_ceiling` / `watchdog` frame from the trip that halted the session, which is deliberate and unchanged); the classifier's other consumer is the terminal emit, which a guardrail error cannot reach, because the in-flight arms halt via `Interrupt()` and the turn comes back cancelled. So this is the metrics half only, and the two guardrail kinds now appear on dashboards keyed to `error.type`. **Left open deliberately:** [#818](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/818) also reports that an in-flight guardrail halt emits *two* `turn-error` frames against a spec that promises exactly one, and auditing that turned up a second variant the issue does not mention — a ceiling that trips in the *post-turn* hook emits its frame from inside the same cleanup that then emits `turn-complete`, which is the same violation with the opposite remedy, since there the turn genuinely did succeed and the trip is not its outcome. The two variants disagree about which frame is the wrong one, which is the issue's own root cause showing through: a guardrail halt is not modelled as a turn outcome. Resolving it means either a non-terminal event type for trips (a protocol addition) or picking a side; that is a maintainer call, not a fix to land quietly, so the frame behaviour is untouched here and the issue stays open for it. ([#818](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/818))
- **A detached subagent's result was pushed into a queue nothing was scheduled to read; a background report now wakes the parent, and reports the queue had to discard now reach the model.** `spawn_agent { wait: true }` caps its wait (5 minutes by default) and, on timeout, tells the model the subagent is "still running in background, result will be pushed". Delivery was a **pull**: `Agent.Run` calls `PrependPendingAlerts` at the top of each turn, which drains the manager's alert channel into a `[Background reports]` block. Nothing pushed and nothing woke the parent, so the promise held only while the parent happened to keep taking turns — and detaching is self-selecting for the case where it doesn't, because a wait times out precisely when the subagent is slow, which is when the parent has most likely already wrapped up. `auto_continue` ([#559](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/559)) and `schedule_next_turn` usually rescued it, but neither is triggered *by* the pending report; they just happened to run one. Fixed by making the enqueue a push: `pushAlert` now fires the parent's wake signal after a successful send, so a supervisor parked in `SleepScheduler.BeforeNextTurn`, an attach-mode daemon in `runner.WakeLoop`, or a REPL sitting at its prompt starts a turn and drains the report instead of waiting for its next scheduled one. This is the shape `wake.go`'s own doc comment already described and that `dev/uat/scheduled-monitor` had been wiring by hand through `OnAlert` + `autonomous.Handle.RequestWake` — the runtime now does it, and the doc comment no longer claims nothing in-tree does. `RequestWake` rather than the unexported fire, deliberately: it also publishes a `wake` frame, and since nothing else in `pkg/agent/background` reaches the wire at all, that frame is the only evidence an attached operator has that a child's report moved the parent (the inbox path fires silently for the opposite reason — it publishes its own frame a few lines earlier). **Every** alert wakes, not only terminal ones: a supervisor asleep on a 30-minute timer should hear a child's mid-run finding, the trade is stated rather than glossed: the wake signal is buffered-1 per subscriber, so several reports arriving during one parent turn coalesce into a single wake, but a steady trickle across idle time now costs a parent turn apiece where before it cost nothing until something else started one. That is the intended behaviour (a report the parent never reads is a report that didn't happen) and the parent's own cost ceiling and watchdog are the backstop; there is deliberately no knob, since one would be an operator-facing choice between "reports arrive" and "reports are cheap" and nothing has asked for the second. A wake fired in the instant a turn drains the queue can also leave the driver one empty turn to find nothing pending — the window is between the channel send and the fire, so nanoseconds wide, and the alternative ordering trades it for the failure that actually matters, an alert landing with no wake behind it. Second half: the alert queue is bounded (256) and drops the **oldest** on overflow, and pre-fix the only trace was a `log.Printf` to the daemon's stderr. The model — the one reader that could react, by re-asking a subagent or checking `list_agents` — read a truncated report list with no way to know it was truncated. Evictions are now counted and surfaced as a synthetic leading entry in the next `[Background reports]` block, naming how many were lost and what to do about it; it leads because the discarded reports are older than every survivor, and the counter is read after the drain and cleared, so a rival consumer draining `Alerts()` first can't swallow the notice and a later turn isn't told twice. The timeout message now says *where* the result lands ("pushed into your next turn"), which is what lets a model end its turn and read it rather than padding the turn out to stay alive. **Out of scope, stated rather than implied:** the queue is in memory, so a restart still drops what is pending, and an explicitly-named re-spawn still discards the original's report via the [#488](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/488) name-reuse suppression (auto-derived instance names make ordinary retries safe). Both are recorded in the docs as limits rather than left to be re-found. ([#780](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/780))
- **`spawn_agent` was documented as plan-gated in four places and routed through no gate at all; it calls the gate now.** Under `plan_mode: "required"`, `record_plan`'s own description told the model *"call this BEFORE any … `spawn_agent` call, or those calls are denied"* — and then `record_plan`'s result, which since [#747](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/747) enumerates the set the gate is really asked about, declined to list `spawn_agent` among what the plan unblocked. Two strings contradicting each other inside one turn, observable in all seven incident sessions of the 2026-08-19 GKE run. The enforcement was also the exact inverse of what the plan-first design's Q3 specified: `pkg/agent/background` consulted no gate, while a child holding MCP tools had to record its own plan before its first call (`mcp` is not plan-exempt) — so the child couldn't act without a plan and creating the child was free. `spawn_agent` and `spawn_remote_agent` now call `gate.CheckGeneric` before anything launches, which brings plan-first, the allow/deny policy and the ask prompt to the act of delegating. The key a rule matches is the subagent, not the goal (a goal is fresh prose every call and could never match a rule twice): `spawn_agent:cluster` withholds one catalog entry, `spawn_agent:ad-hoc:*` refuses inline personas whatever the model names them, `spawn_agent:*` refuses delegation outright. A denial renders as a refusal, not a launch — same `error` key as every other failed spawn ([#746](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/746)) — and the spawn tools register themselves with the gate as they are built, so `record_plan`'s result now names `spawn_agent` too. **A declarative subagent has two doors and both are gated**, which the issue does not mention and which a fix to `spawn_agent` alone would have missed entirely: `agent.WithSubagents` also materialises each one as a named synchronous parent tool, so a parent held back from `spawn_agent {agent: "cluster"}` could still have called `cluster(request: …)` and got the same delegate by the other name. `agent.SubagentOptions` gains a `Gate`, filled in from the parent's `agent.WithGate` — the one place these tools are constructed, and therefore the only place a gate can reach them — and it matches under the **same** `spawn_agent` bucket, because an operator who wrote `deny: ["spawn_agent:cluster"]` meant that `cluster` does not run, not that it does not run asynchronously. One rule, both doors. `WithGate`'s documented "metadata-only" contract is amended accordingly: it is still metadata for every other tool, and now also the gate the subagent tools are built with. **`stop_agent` is deliberately left ungated**: it cancels, so every denial of it leaves running exactly what the model was trying to halt, and plan-exempting it would have fixed only the `plan_mode` case while leaving an `allow`-mode config unable to stop anything. Withholding the tool ([#748](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/748)) remains how an operator says "delegate but don't cancel". ([#758](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/758))
- **Auto-continue answered a question twice: the in-lifetime retry scan read a turn that was still generating as a turn that had been interrupted. `*agent.Agent` gains `TurnInFlight`.** On a live `v2.9.0-dev` daemon fronting a chat gateway, one user message at `20:25:12` produced two replies — the model's own at `20:25:34` after ~22 seconds of generation, and a second turn queued at `20:25:22` by a retry tick that declared the turn "interrupted 10s ago" while it was mid-flight, then ran the moment the real answer landed. The cause is a limitation of the classifier that only the retry driver could expose: `ClassifyInterruptedTail` reads committed history, and **an interrupted turn and a running one leave the same tail** — the user's message with no model reply yet. On the boot path that ambiguity does not exist (nothing is in flight after a restart), which is why the classifier was sound for the caller it was written for; [#575](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/575)'s in-lifetime retry driver then re-ran the same pass on a timer, where it is the common case, and [#559](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/559) made auto-continue default-on for exactly the daemons this hits. Exposure is roughly turn duration over `retry_interval` — the issue's "any turn slower than the retry interval" reads too narrowly against its own evidence, since a ~22-second turn is not slower than any plausible interval; a tick simply landed in it, and the longer the answer the likelier that is. The existing `AcquireLock` guard does not cover it and never claimed to: the eventlog run lock is *fleet* mutual exclusion, taken by `autonomous.Resume` and by auto-continue itself, and an ordinary attach-driven or wake-loop turn holds no lease — which is why the live log shows no "run lock held elsewhere" line and the scan acquired the lock uncontended on a session that was busy answering. Liveness is a fact the process holds rather than one history records, so the injector now asks for it: `Agent.TurnInFlight()` exports the same registration `Interrupt()` acts on, and `lockClassifyInject` stands down on it with a refundable outcome (no note was injected; the running turn commits its own reply), covering all three triggers rather than just the scan. Three things make that signal exact rather than approximate. It **brackets the ambiguous window**: registration happens in `Run` before the runner is handed the turn's message and the clear happens in the post-drain cleanup, so every event the runner commits for the turn — the opening user message included — is written while it reports true, and the ambiguous tail can never be read with the check false. It is **per-agent, hence per-session**: the reported daemon was multi-session, and a process-wide "something is running" flag would have suppressed continuations for every idle session while one was busy. And it is checked **after** classification, not before, so a turn that starts between the two reads is caught by the check instead of injected against — the reverse order clears the check and then classifies the user message the new turn just committed, which is the same bug one instruction apart. The fix sits at the caller deliberately: a pure function over `[]*session.Event` cannot honestly answer "is this still running", and the alternative on offer — a time-based heuristic like "a trailing user event newer than N seconds is probably still generating" — is a guess in the position where guessing wrong duplicates a reply to a human, and the interruption timestamp is the *user* event's, so it is arbitrarily recent for precisely the long turns at risk. `ClassifyInterruptedTail`'s doc comment now carries the limitation and names `TurnInFlight` as the fact to consult, so the next caller meets it at the classifier rather than in production. **Scope, stated plainly:** the check is in-process, so a peer daemon's turn against a shared eventlog still reports false — not a regression (nothing guarded that case before either: the two callers that take the run lock today are `autonomous.Resume` and auto-continue, and a plain turn is neither), but the cross-daemon shape of this bug remains open, and closing it needs a durable per-turn marker whose cost — a write on every turn's hot path plus a stale-lease story for SIGKILL — is not worth paying for a shape nobody has observed. There was no live UAT of this fix: the analysis is source plus the issue's log evidence, and the regression test constructs the situation directly — a registered agent holding a real turn open on a blocking model, scanned by the pass the retry driver runs, with the same session and the same tail continued normally when no turn is running. ([#796](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/796))

- **The local `--tui` adapter handed core-tui the agent's own wake channel, so an operator toast and a sleeping scheduler took turns consuming wakes. `*agent.Agent` gains `SubscribeWake`.** `agent.wakeSignal` was one buffered-1 channel and `WakeRequested()` returned it to everybody, so each wake was delivered to exactly one receiver. `cmd/core-agent/coretui_enabled.go` forwarded that channel straight to core-tui's `WakeRequester` subscription — which put the TUI's listener and the agent's driver on the same one-shot value, and whichever reached it first consumed it. Silent in both directions: a sleep that should have been interrupted just runs to term, a toast that should have appeared just doesn't. This is the hazard [#802](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/802) sidestepped on the attach side by feeding `coretuiremote` from a `wake` SSE frame instead of subscribing; local mode still had the original shape. `wakeSignal` is now a fan-out: `SubscribeWake()` returns an independent buffered-1, drop-on-full channel plus an idempotent unsubscribe, and every fire reaches every subscriber with one non-blocking send apiece. Coalescing is unchanged and now per subscriber — a wake still means "re-check state", never "process exactly N events", and one slow reader drops only its own redundant wakes. **Scope, stated precisely, because the issue's framing is wider than what shipped broken:** five non-test call sites reach `WakeRequested()` — the autonomous driver's two (`autonomous.go` + `resume.go`, both via `tools.ContextWithWake` into `SleepScheduler`), `runner.REPL`'s between-turn select, `runner.WakeLoop`, and this adapter. Read against `main`, they cannot currently hold the *same* agent's channel at once: `cmd/core-agent`'s mode branches (`--prompt` / `--no-repl` / `--tui` / REPL) each return before the next, multi-session wake loops each get their own factory-built agent, and every in-tree `autonomous.Run` (background subagents, declarative subagents) builds a fresh agent inside its own `build` closure. So this is a latent API defect rather than a reachable runtime race today, and only the TUI adapter moves — the three drivers keep `WakeRequested()`, whose contract is now documented as exactly that: the *driver's* subscription, one per agent, stable for the agent's lifetime. Stability is load-bearing rather than incidental — core-tui re-invokes `WakeRequested` to re-arm its listener after every wake and the autonomous driver re-invokes it on every turn that schedules a successor, so a method that minted a subscription per call would leak one per wake and strand any wake latched in the discarded one; the adapter therefore subscribes exactly once and keeps the same channel. Teardown covers the whole session, not just one adapter: `/model` swaps in a *new* adapter and core-tui re-arms its listener against whatever is in `m.opts.Agent` at the time, so a post-swap wake subscribes the successor — which `launchTUIv2`'s deferred release would never have seen. Every adapter in a session therefore registers its unsubscribe with one shared group, and a subscription taken after the group closes is released on the spot. Unsubscribing deregisters without closing: closing races a concurrent fire (send on a closed channel panics), and a closed channel also reads to core-tui as a permanently-ended subscription — it stops re-arming — where a quiet one is what's wanted. `subscribe`/unsubscribe republish the subscriber set copy-on-write behind an atomic pointer, so `fire` is a lock-free load plus one non-blocking send apiece and stays safe on the loop's hot path, and the eagerly-allocated default subscription keeps a wake fired before any reader arrives latched — the ordering `pkg/compose`'s auto-continue depends on (inject first, wake loop second). This converges the two TUI hosts on one wake *contract* — a stable per-adapter buffered-1 channel — but not on one implementation: the remote adapter's feed is an SSE frame because its agent is in another process, and having the attach broadcaster subscribe to the fan-out instead is deliberately not done, since `Inject` fires the signal without publishing a `wake` event and a subscription-driven emitter would announce every typed prompt as an attention notice. ([#813](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/813))

- **otel: an inject and the turn it causes were two unrelated traces, and the docs both claimed otherwise and sent readers chasing a phantom `traceparent` bug.** The cross-process hop was never broken — a live GKE run shows the watcher's client span with the daemon's `POST /sessions/{sid}/inject` as a genuine child, `service.name=lookout-watch` → `service.name=core-agent`. What broke is everything after: `/inject` queues the message and returns, its span ends, and the turn that answers it minutes later got handed a context with no span on it, so ADK's `invoke_agent` — which inherits whatever parent is on the context it's given — opened a brand-new trace root with nothing tying it to the request that asked for it (62 spans, `parent: None`, on the live run). The docs called this chain "one trace across two processes" and then listed three causes to chase when you inevitably saw two, while the span tree three lines below already marked `invoke_agent` as a root. `Agent.Run` now opens core-agent's own `agent.turn` span on the per-turn context **before** handing it to the runner, which makes ADK's span its child with no ADK fork — we own the context, and that is the whole mechanism. Injects reach it as **span links**, not a parent edge, and that is the load-bearing design decision rather than an implementation shortcut: injects batch, one turn routinely answers several, and a parent edge would have to pick one and misattribute the turn to it — asynchronous fan-in is what links are specified for. New `Agent.InjectAsContext` captures the injecting call's `trace.SpanContext` onto the queued message (the same path the inbox already threads the caller identity down), and `Inject` / `InjectAs` stay exactly as they were, delegating to it; the attach handlers reach it through a new **optional** `attach.ContextInjector`, because widening the exported `attach.Registrant` interface would break every out-of-tree implementation for a telemetry improvement, and a registrant that doesn't implement it simply produces no link. `POST /wake` with a prompt is an inject and gets the same treatment. Untraced injects — CLI, library callers, auto-continue's own notes — are a clean no-op: the capture is a context-value read of a value type, so it allocates nothing when tracing is off, and a wholly untraced batch produces no links and no attribute. Links are capped at 32 per turn against an inbox that holds 256, keeping the **newest** since the turn originator is the last caller in the batch, with the pre-cap count preserved on `core_agent.inbox.linked_injects`. The docs page is rewritten around the real shape, including the one that cost the most time to establish: **`service.name` is not a usable filter on the Cloud Trace v1 REST API** — `+service_name:core-agent` returns zero traces on a cluster that is exporting correctly, because it keys off the legacy per-trace service concept rather than the OTel resource attribute. `url.path:`, `gen_ai.conversation.id:` and `k8s.namespace.name:` all work and are verified against live data. ([#806](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/806))

- **TUI: the local `--tui` adapter's `Interrupt` satisfied no interface at all, and a doc comment naming a type that has never existed is what kept anyone from noticing.** `cmd/core-agent`'s in-process adapter carried `func (a *coreAgentAdapter) Interrupt() bool` under the comment *"satisfies coretui.Interruptible"*. There is no `coretui.Interruptible` — not in v0.22.0, not in any release; the name appears to have been invented in `docs/core-tui-adapter-design.md`'s binding table and copied from there into the code, where it read as a conformance claim. The real capability is `RemoteInterrupter`, `Interrupt(ctx context.Context) error`, and core-tui discovers it by **type assertion**, which is why the mismatch was never a build error: the assertion declined, `/interrupt` fell past the capability arm to the terminal "no turn in flight" row, and the method rode six core-tui pin bumps (v0.15.0 → v0.22.0) looking healthy. Attach mode was never affected — `internal/coretuiremote` has had the right signature with a `var _` to prove it since [#304](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/304). The method is now `Interrupt(ctx context.Context) error`, and `var _ coretui.RemoteInterrupter = (*coreAgentAdapter)(nil)` joins the exhaustive guard list [#810](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/810) built in `cmd/core-agent/coretui_guards.go` — moving up out of that file's *deliberately-not-implemented* section, where it had been parked with the note that the guard "would not compile today". **The guard is the actual fix**: a prose claim of conformance that nothing checks is the bug class here, not a footnote to it. That is also why the two design docs carrying the phantom name are corrected in the same change, and why the not-implemented entry is deleted rather than left to read as a standing decision. Worth being exact about what this buys locally, since overstating it would repeat the mistake: core-tui reaches `RemoteInterrupter` only after its `state == stateStreaming && cancelTurn != nil` gate declines, this host sets and clears both together and is not a `LiveAgent`, so locally the arm is reached at idle or in the window where the TUI has finalized a turn `inner` is still unwinding. The turn-stopping win today is small; the change earns its place because a capability method that satisfies nothing is a defect either way, and because [core-tui#260](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/260)'s ESC cascade dispatches through `RemoteInterrupter` unconditionally and would be a no-op locally until this shape is right. `agent.Agent.Interrupt() bool` keeps its shape and **false maps to an error**, because core-tui's contract reads nil as "the cancel landed" — it prints *"remote turn cancelled"* and calls `endLiveStretch()` — and claiming a cancel that didn't happen is not on offer. That has a visible cost, taken deliberately: at idle the operator now sees core-tui's *"cancelling remote turn…"* placeholder followed by a red `RoleError` row, where one plain system row with the same text used to do. Two honest rows beat one row and a lie; the placeholder's remote-flavoured wording is core-tui's to fix. `ctx` is deliberately unused — the in-process cancel is a local function-pointer call with no I/O for core-tui's 5s bound to guard, unlike the attach adapter which spends that ctx on an HTTP round-trip. Cancel-only, no hold: the re-drive a hold would defend against isn't armed here, since core-tui runs auto-continue off `turnDoneMsg` while a cancelled turn lands on `turnCancelledMsg` and reaches only `maybeDrainQueue`, which starts prompts the operator typed themselves. ([#803](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/803))
- **Attach-mode wake notifications have never fired, and fixing the method name would not have fixed that. The attach protocol goes 1.6.0 → 1.7.0.** `internal/coretuiremote` carried a `RequestWake()` whose comment said it satisfied `coretui.WakeRequester` and served an operator `/wake` slash. Every clause of that was false: the interface is `WakeRequested() <-chan struct{}`, it is a **push** channel the TUI subscribes to once at startup rather than a command the TUI calls, and no `/wake` slash has existed in any core-tui release. core-tui feature-detects by type assertion with no error and no log, so the subscription was simply never made and the toast never appeared — since the capability was added. Renaming the method (the fix the issue proposed) would have made it worse: it would compile, satisfy a compile guard, and still never fire, because **there was no `wake` frame in the attach protocol for it to receive** — the wake happens daemon-side, and `supportedEventTypes` had no way to say so. So the frame is what actually ships here. `*agent.Agent.RequestWake` now publishes a `wake` event alongside firing the signal, emitted **from the agent rather than the attach handler** for the same reason `pause` is: `POST /wake` is the only wake path in a stock daemon that goes through HTTP at all, and the one that matters more — a host draining `BackgroundAgentManager.Alerts()` into `autonomous.Handle.RequestWake`, the shape `dev/uat/scheduled-monitor` demonstrates — never touches a handler, so a handler-side emit would hide it from precisely the operator who cannot see the process. (That host wiring is a door, not a default: nothing in `cmd/` or `pkg/` installs it, and the docs now say so rather than describing it as something the daemon does.) Emitting was the only option available, not a style preference — the wake *channel* is single-consumer with a buffer of one, so a broadcaster subscribing to it would steal wakes from the autonomous scheduler the signal exists to interrupt. `Inject` deliberately does **not** produce a wake frame even though it fires the same signal internally: it already announces itself as an `inbox` frame, and a wake on top would make every prompt an operator types raise an attention notice about their own typing. The payload is only `at`, because the thing that did the waking reports itself through its own frames and a `reason` field nothing on this side can fill is a promise to consumers the producer can't keep. Client side, `attachclient` parses the frame and the adapter hands it to core-tui on a buffered-1, drop-on-full, never-closed channel — non-blocking because that send happens on the SSE read loop that also carries model output, and freezing the transcript is a strictly worse outcome than dropping a redundant "look now" (the agent's own wake signal coalesces identically, so this is the same guarantee local mode already gives). Detection is `"wake"` in the capabilities frame's `event_types`; a pre-1.7.0 daemon omits it, sends no frame, and a 1.7.0 client degrades to the silence it had before rather than to an error. One caveat worth knowing before you enable this in front of operators: core-tui v0.22.0 answers a wake with a toast **and** a permanent system row asserting that "an external alert … is waiting in the inbox", which is true for the alert-wired host case and an over-claim for a bare `POST /wake`. It can't be fixed from this side — `coretui.WakeRequester` is a `<-chan struct{}`, so no `reason` this end could add would ever reach the renderer — and local sessions have shown the same row for the same reason since the capability landed, so attach mode is reaching parity with local rather than inventing a new wrongness. Both reference pages carry the caution. The `/wake` slash the docs advertised is removed from `attach-tui.md` — it never existed to remove from the code. ([#802](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/802))
- **`${env:VAR}` silently did nothing when a bundle shipped no `.agents/env.yaml`, and the placeholder reached the model as literal persona text. The daemon now names what survived, at boot.** Every link in the chain was correct on its own: `LoadManifest` returns nil when the optional manifest is absent, `NewResolver(nil, ...)` returns a nil `*Resolver`, `InterpolateFunc()` on nil returns nil, and every loader documents nil as "no interpolation" and passes bodies through untouched — the parent's instruction files and skills, and each declarative subagent's own instructions, root `AGENTS.md` and root `skills/` tree. Composed, that is a no-op that announces nothing, and `${env:GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT}` arrives in the system prompt looking to the model exactly like a value it was given. (`mcp.json` was never affected and is not in scope here: its `env` / `headers` values go through the manifest-unaware `agentenv.InterpolateEnv`, so they interpolate from the ambient process env with or without a manifest — which is part of why the instruction-side silence was so hard to place.) [#712](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/712) reports it propagating into a live GKE tool call as `projects/${env:GOOGLE_CLOUD_PROJECT}/locations/${env:GKE_LOCATION}/clusters/${env:GKE_CLUSTER}`, the API rejecting it, and the agent then hunting for its own identity — `read_file /proc/self/environ`, re-reading its own `AGENTS.md` — with every failed attempt growing a transcript that every later turn re-sends: 22 turns and 752K input tokens for $0.73 before it was interrupted, against $0.26 for the healthy comparison run. What makes it worth a changelog entry longer than the fix is the misdiagnosis it invites, and did: the identity-hunting reads as a persona weakness, was once "fixed" as one by forbidding the agent to read `/proc/self/environ`, and that removed the only workaround it had. **Detection reads the loaded content, not the manifest's absence.** A new `agentenv.ResidualRefs` wraps the interpolator — non-nil even when the resolver is nil, which is the entire mechanism, since a nil interpolator is precisely the one loaders skip — and records any `${env:...}` still present in each body after the pass ran. Content is the right thing to watch for two reasons: a bundle with no manifest and no placeholders is healthy and must stay quiet, which is most bundles; and a bundle **with** an active manifest can still ship a placeholder the interpolator will never match, like `${env:my-var}`, which no manifest-shaped check could ever catch and which the existing drift report is structurally blind to (it reports names the interpolator *saw*, and this one is never seen). The two cases get different text, because the remedy differs: no manifest says `interpolation is OFF` and names the directory the manifest would have been read from; an active manifest says the name isn't a valid `${env:NAME}` reference. Surviving placeholders are quoted verbatim so an operator can grep their bundle for the exact string, capped at twelve with an exact total. The line is emitted **after** the declarative subagents are built rather than beside the existing drift report, because a rooted subagent loads its own content root at that point and the issue's evidence came from exactly that shape. **Warning, not fatal**, and the boundary is a real one rather than caution: `${env:...}` appears legitimately as literal text in content that *documents* the feature, two of the three skill bundles this repo ships under `SKILLS/` quote it inside example `mcp.json` blocks, and `SKILLS/README.md` tells operators to copy those bundles straight into `.agents/skills/` — so refusing to boot on a surviving placeholder would turn a documentation string into an outage. There is no suppression flag; adding one would recreate the silence this exists to break. Nothing about interpolation itself changes: a manifest still substitutes exactly what it substituted before, the manifest stays optional, and the issue's third suggestion — falling back to ambient `os.Getenv` when no manifest exists — is deliberately **not** taken, since splicing arbitrary process environment into the system prompt of a bundle that never opted in is a larger change than the diagnostic it would replace, and the discontinuity it describes (a zero-entry manifest interpolates everything, no manifest interpolates nothing) is now something the daemon says out loud instead of something a recipe author has to predict. The two recipes in `examples/` that use `${env:` were audited and both already carry a manifest reachable from their agents dir. ([#712](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/712))

- **Attach TUI: a failed first fetch was cached as though it were an answer, so the status header and the usage footer served placeholders for a full TTL after the daemon came back.** All three cached read-only surfaces in `internal/coretuiremote` — the status header, the usage footer and the subagent roster — are stale-while-revalidate: serve the last-known-good value, revalidate in the background, and bump `lastFetch` even when the fetch fails so a down daemon isn't hammered from the render path. Bumping on error is right in itself; what was missing was any record of whether the cached value was *real*, so "we asked and failed" was indistinguishable from "we asked and the answer was empty". Attach while a daemon is mid-restart and the first request fails, and the zero value — `—` / `(model not set)`, a row of zeros — was pinned for the whole 2s TTL even if the daemon recovered a moment later. [#778](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/778) hit this where it was not cosmetic: the roster's zero value renders as the affirmative sentence "no subagents running", so one dropped request made core-tui state something false and the TTL held it there. It was fixed locally, in the roster's own cache struct, and the other two were left with the original shape — which is the divergence [#781](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/781) was filed on, and the reason this is a shape fix rather than two one-line patches. The bookkeeping is now one embedded `swrState` shared by all three caches, carrying the `haveGood` flag (only a successful fetch promotes the cache to holding data) and a single `swrState.next` that returns what the accessor should do — serve, fetch inline, or revalidate — so the freshness policy lives in one place and the next capability that needs caching inherits the fixed shape rather than the broken one. While no successful fetch has landed, the retry interval is the short `coldRetryTTL` (250ms) rather than the surface's steady-state TTL, clamped so it can never be the *longer* of the two. **Warm behaviour is deliberately unchanged**: once a value is good, a later failure keeps the steady-state cadence, so a daemon that dies mid-session is still asked every 2s and not four times a second. The cost is stated rather than glossed: extending the cold interval from one surface to three triples the worst case, a daemon that is down at attach and never returns being asked ~4×/s per surface — bounded by the per-cache in-flight guard (at most one outstanding request each), off the render goroutine, and over at the first success. Attaching to a permanently dead daemon is not the case worth optimising for; recovering promptly from one that is down for the first second is. ([#781](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/781))

- **`GET /sessions/{sid}/tools` reported neither MCP tools nor skill tools — not misclassified, absent — so clients guessed server attribution by splitting tool names on `_`.** `attach.ToolInfo` has carried `Source` and `Server` since it was defined, and the production adapter set `Source` to one of `builtin` / `subagent` / `other` and `Server` never. [#767](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/767) reads that as MCP and skill tools landing in `other`; the truth was worse and is what made the fix bigger than a classification pass. `AttachTools` projects `agent.Tools()`, which is the `agent.WithTools` slice — built-ins plus the synchronous subagent tools. MCP servers and the skill bundle arrive through `agent.WithToolsets` and are handed straight to `llmagent.Config.Toolsets`; the agent never merges the two, and does not retain the toolsets at all. So every MCP tool and all three skill tools were missing from the endpoint entirely, and had been since it shipped. Downstream, [mast-web#44](https://github.com/go-steer/mast-web/issues/44) shipped a `/mcp` command that prefers `source === 'mcp' && server` and otherwise infers the server from the tool name's first underscore-delimited segment — a heuristic that invents servers out of built-ins containing an underscore and collapses servers whose tools aren't prefixed, written that way only because there was no correct alternative. The catalog is now assembled from the three places the agent actually keeps it: `agent.Tools()` as before, plus the MCP snapshot behind `WithMCPProvider` and a new `WithSkillToolsProvider`. Reading the **snapshot** rather than enumerating the live toolsets is the deliberate call — `mcp.Server.ToolInfos` is materialised once at startup, so `/tools` stays a pure in-memory read instead of fanning out a `tools/list` round-trip per server on an operator keystroke (and hanging on a wedged server), and `/tools` and `/mcp` cannot disagree about which server owns what, because they read the same value. Skills needed the new provider because the bundle is not one tool per skill: the ADK skill toolset registers a small fixed set (`list_skills` / `load_skill` / `load_skill_resource`) that the model calls with a skill *name* as an argument, so the existing `WithSkillsProvider` — which lists the installed skills — is the wrong list, and `skills.Skills.ToolInfos()` is the right one. Both composition roots wire it: the single-session CLI and `pkg/compose`'s session factory. The `gate_state` column needed care that the tool name alone would have got wrong: `tools.GateToolset` routes every call through `CheckToolCall` with the **namespace** (`mcp` / `skill`) as the tool name — the underlying name only keys per-tool session grants — so an `mcp:*` deny rule matches on `mcp` and never on `gke_list_clusters`. Projecting `ToolGateState` off the underlying name would have rendered "prompted" on every row of a surface the gate refuses outright, which is a column contradicting the next call rather than merely an imprecise one. Name collisions resolve first-writer-wins with `agent.Tools()` first, so two providers claiming one name produce one row rather than an ambiguous key. Unwired providers contribute nothing rather than erroring, matching the rest of the `Attach*` surface. The embedded TUI's adapter also picks up the `Server`-into-`Source` flattening `internal/coretuiremote` already did, so `--tui` and a remote client attached to the same daemon render `/tools` identically. ([#767](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/767))

#### Feature

- **`core-tui` v0.22.0 → v0.24.0: Esc holds a remote agent, `/mouse` sticks, and `/permissions` can finally say who approved.** Three capabilities the daemon has been ready for and the pinned client could not express, plus two renames. **The hold** ([core-tui#260](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/260), landed in the skipped v0.23.0) is `coretui.Pauser`, and `internal/coretuiremote` implements it: Esc against a daemon-driven agent cancels the turn and *then* shuts the session's gate, so "stop and wait for me" is what the key finally does instead of ending this client's subscription while the daemon carries the turn through to the end. Held is not idle — an idle agent picks up the next queued prompt on its own and a held one starts nothing until you say so — so the TUI banners the distinction, and there are three ways out: type to steer, `/continue`, `/abandon`. Nothing new is needed daemon-side; the whole surface (`POST /pause`, `POST /resume`, the `pause` frame, the folded pause block on `GET /status`) has shipped since protocol 1.5.0, and the adapter's vocabulary is a field-for-field copy rather than a translation. The state is **seeded once** from `/status` at attach and thereafter written only by the stream: a session already held before you connected had its transition before your `?since=` cursor, so the poll is the only thing that can see it, while letting the 1 Hz refresh keep writing would race a resume and flash the banner back on. The local `--tui` host **declines** `Pauser`, recorded as a `//coretui:declined` with its reason: local mode is the per-turn `Run` path, nothing starts a turn but the operator pressing Enter, so "no new turn starts until someone resumes" is already unconditional and a gate in front of it would gate nothing — and Esc correctly keeps falling through to the local cancel. If local mode ever grows an autonomous driver, that decline is the first thing to revisit. **`/mouse` now persists** ([core-tui#287](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/287)) through the new `Options.PersistMouseChoice` hook and `config.PersistMouseChoice`, which writes `ui.mouse` the way `/theme` writes `ui.theme`. This is the half [#859](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/859) left open when `--no-mouse` shipped for the attach client: mouse capture takes away native click-drag text selection for as long as the TUI runs, and without a durable toggle an operator who wanted selection back re-typed `/mouse` every single launch. The value is always written **explicitly**, never cleared — `UI.Mouse` is a tristate whose nil means "use the default" and the default is on, so a toggle-off that cleared the field would persist the exact opposite of what was asked for; there is a test that reads the raw file for the key. v0.24.0 also emits a five-second hint saying why selection stopped working, naming the bypass modifier only where `TERM_PROGRAM` identifies the terminal — which is the same conclusion #859 reached from the other end. **`/permissions` names the approver** ([core-tui#277](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/277)): [#830](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/830) taught the gate to attribute approvals and both adapters have had the identity in hand ever since, dropping it at the `coretui.ApprovalLog{…}` literal because the field did not exist upstream. It does now, and both adapters fill it. Empty stays empty — a local terminal, where the answerer is whoever is at the keyboard, and an unauthenticated listener both render exactly as they did before, because a placeholder in an audit line is indistinguishable at a glance from a name somebody verified. Two renames land with the bump and are documented rather than merely absorbed: `/resume` is **`/transcripts`** (the word belonged to `POST /resume`, the endpoint behind `/continue` and `/abandon`, while the command wearing it listed files off local disk — the old spelling folds until core-tui v1.0), and `/tools` groups by source with a per-group count and takes a source to filter on, which is the difference between reading your own built-ins and scrolling past someone's 31-tool MCP server. Two stale cautions in `reference/attach-http.md` are corrected in the same pass, both describing client defects the bump fixes: the wake row no longer asserts that something is waiting in the inbox, and a cancelled turn no longer offers a `↻ retryable` affordance against a retry action core-tui has never had. `verify-coretui-guards` is what forced the accounting — it failed the bump on `Pauser` being in neither list, which is precisely the drift it was built for ([#812](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/812)); the tree now reads 22 exported interfaces, 31 guards and 13 declines. ([#859](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/859))

- **`mock.NewScriptedPerCall` — a scripted provider that hands every `Model` call its own replay, so a hermetic fan-out is a constructor rather than a workaround.** `mock.NewScripted` returns one `*scriptedLLM` from every `Model` call, and that LLM has a single cursor. That is right for a single agent whose transcript is the whole conversation, and wrong for a `background.Manager`, which asks its provider for one LLM per spawn: three concurrent children walk one script between them, so child A replays recorded turn 0, child B turn 1, child C turn 2, and which is which depends on goroutine scheduling. The cursor is mutex-guarded, which makes this **worse than a data race, not better** — `-race` stays silent while the replay is nondeterministic, and the first symptom is usually a `script exhausted` from whichever child lost. `examples/parallel-spawn` had been carrying a local six-line `models.Provider` to work around it; that is deleted and the example uses the constructor. The transcript is read and parsed once, at construction, so a malformed one fails there rather than at the first spawn and an edit to the file mid-run cannot change what a later spawn replays; each `Model` call then re-parses those same bytes rather than sharing the decoded turns, because recorded responses are pointers handed straight into the agent loop and two concurrent children must not be given the same ones. `NewScripted` is unchanged, and its doc now says which of the two you want. Two API-shape notes from the same report ride along: `attach.createSessionResponse.URL` is documented as *conditionally* absolute — scheme + host echoed from the request's `Host` header, degrading to a bare path only when there is no `Host`, so it is neither reliably a path nor reliably absolute and a client must use it as-is rather than prefixing its own base — and the third item, the CSRF `415` on bodyless POSTs, needed no change: `pkg/attach/csrf.go` already spells out "add the header even when the request has no body" and the attach-protocol reference already documents it in four places. ([#476](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/476))

- **`edit_file` gains an opt-in `replace_all`, and the refusal that used to be a dead end now names it.** The tool has always been single-occurrence-only: a snippet matching more than once is refused, on the reasoning that ambiguity usually means the model has not read enough of the file to know which site it wants. That default is kept — `replace_all` defaults to false and an omitted flag behaves exactly as before — but with no opt-in at all, a rename across twelve call sites was twelve rounds of widening each snippet until it happened to be unique, which is a lot of turns spent proving something the caller already knew. **The refusal now names the way out**, quoting both the match count and the flag: a capability the model cannot see is a capability it does not have, which is the [#759](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/759)/[#762](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/762) rule pointed the other way — there the fix was to stop advertising what the runtime could not do, here it is to start advertising what it can. `replace_all` relaxes "must occur exactly once" to "must occur at least once": a missing string is still an error in both modes, so a rename cannot report success having changed nothing. It is an opt-out of the uniqueness *check*, not a mode switch — a unique match behaves identically with the flag set, so a model that passes it defensively gets no surprises. The occurrence count goes in the `status` string as well as `replacements`, because the status is the part a model reliably reads and "that touched seven places" is precisely the fact the caller could not know before asking — a count larger than expected is how an over-broad snippet announces itself. Matches are consumed left to right, so overlapping ones (`aa` in `aaaa`) count as two and not three. The gate is unmoved: `CheckFileWrite` runs before `old_string` is ever counted, so the new argument cannot widen what a path scope allows. A multi-edit batch — the other half of the issue's "and/or" — is deliberately not included: it is a new tool surface with its own atomicity question (does edit 3 of 5 failing roll back the first two?), and it should be answered on purpose rather than folded into a flag. ([#657](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/657))

- **A declarative subagent can now be given a budget, and it binds on both of the doors the subagent is reachable through.** `subagents[]` gains an optional `budgets` block — `max_turns`, `max_cost_usd`, `max_wallclock_seconds` — capping one delegation along three independent dimensions. `background.SubagentTemplate.Budgets` already existed and was honored downstream; `cmd/core-agent/subagents.go` simply never populated it, and the synchronous door (a subagent installed as a named tool by `agent.WithSubagents`) had no enforcement at all, because until the delegated turns were billed at all ([#849](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/849)) there was no cost signal to compare a dollar limit against. **The two doors ship together deliberately**: the same subagent answers both to a parent tool call and to `spawn_agent { agent: "cluster" }`, so a cap that binds only the door the operator didn't use is worse than no cap — the config reads as though the subagent were bounded and nobody goes looking (the [#759](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/759)/[#762](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/762) antipattern). `syncBudgets` and `asyncBudgets` sit next to each other in `subagents.go` for the same reason one test asserts both projections. On the synchronous side the caps are counted in the tool handler, the way `pkg/agent/subtask.go` counts them for the agentic wrappers, because ADK's `runner.RunConfig` carries only `StreamingMode` and `SaveInputBlobsAsArtifacts` — no turn or cost cap; wall-clock rides a derived context, and its expiry has to be told apart from a genuine run failure, since conflating them would turn a budget stop into a failed tool call — and the label is re-checked once more after the loop, so a runner that ends its iteration cleanly on a cancelled context rather than yielding the error still produces a labelled partial instead of a silently truncated one. **A cap that fires does not fail the delegation**: whatever the subagent produced comes back labelled as a partial, naming the cap and telling the parent to re-delegate the remainder or finish it itself. Discarding the partial was [#691](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/691)'s mistake — the parent paid twice for the same diagnosis — and the label leads rather than trails because a model skimming a long result reads the head, and "this is unfinished" changes how it should read everything after. The cap is checked *after* the turn that trips it is billed, so the ledger and the limit agree. Zero or omitted is unchanged behaviour on both doors: the spawn door keeps falling back to the manager's defaults (50 turns / $1 / 10m) and the tool door stays unbounded. Negative values are a config error rather than clamped, since `0` already means "no cap" and clamping a typo'd `-1` would leave a subagent uncapped while the config said otherwise. Library callers reach the same three dimensions with `agent.WithSubagentBudgets` on the *subagent* — like `WithSubagentMaxDepth`, the cap is a property of the delegate, and a parent with several needs a different one for each — readable back via `(*agent.Agent).DelegationBudgets()`. The block is version-gated in `recipecheck`'s `GatedFeatures` at `2.9.0-dev.4` under the table's "asserts a safety property" rule: `pkg/config` does not set `DisallowUnknownFields`, so an older daemon handed this block drops it and runs the subagent uncapped while the recipe reads as bounded. (Closes the first gap of [#713](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/713))

- **A third loop detector: `dominant-tool-call`, for the interleaved loop the other two converge on slowly or not at all.** `repeated-tool-call` owns a *run* of one call and `alternating-tool-cycle` owns a repeating *sequence*, and the cycle detector explicitly skips blocks made of a single repeated call because the repeat detector already covers them. The shape between them is mostly one repeated call with occasional other calls wedged in: `matches()` tracks a run length that any non-matching call resets to zero, so an interleave restarts the count, and the cycle detector sees a nearly-uniform block and hands it back. In the GKE UAT that filed this the backstop still fired — 22 byte-identical `gke_list_clusters` calls over two minutes and twenty seconds, halted by `repeated-tool-call` at call 22 — so this was a convergence gap, not a miss, and the distinction set the tuning: the loop was visibly degenerate from about the fourth call, and the interleaves were what stretched a threshold of 5 into 22 calls and a large share of a $0.77 session. The new signal trips when one call accounts for **8 of the last 12** observations, which reaches the same verdict inside the first full window because it does not care where the interleaves fall. Args are compared canonicalized (`canonicalArgs`), the same treatment the cycle detector uses and for the same reason — counting occurrences means keying a map, and the repeat detector's pairwise path-suffix relation is not transitive, so it cannot key one.
