Severity is Critical like the other two loop detectors, so it halts under `--watchdog=enforce` and reaches the model under `--watchdog=feedback`. **Adding a third detector to a pool of overlapping ones is mostly a de-duplication problem**, and the one-behavior-one-alert invariant the package already commits to is enforced structurally rather than by tuning luck: the density signal stands down when the dominant call has reached a consecutive run of `DefaultRepeatThreshold` inside the window (that run is the repeat detector's alert to raise) and when the window is a clean repetition of a 2–4 call block (the cycle detector reaches such a window at nine calls, where density needs twelve). Both deferrals are explicit fields rather than hidden behaviour, and zero disables either, because an embedder wiring this signal *without* the other two wants neither. A period longer than the cycle detector scans — `a → a → a → a → b` — matches no deferral and is exactly the case this signal exists for. The deferral does not clear the already-alerted fingerprint: a run or a cycle forming inside a pattern this signal already reported is the same pattern getting worse, not a new one to re-announce. The alert re-arms once activity diversifies below the threshold, which matters because signal state persists across turns and only `ResetWatchdog` clears it — a signal that never re-armed would go permanently silent after one loop in a long session. The known false positive is the cycle detector's, and so is the answer: a poll tight enough to put 8 identical calls in a 12-call window already trips the repeat detector at 5 consecutive unless something interleaves, `wait_and_verify` ([#648](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/648)) is the supported way to wait, and an embedder can construct `watchdog.DefaultWatchdog` with their own signal list. `DefaultRepeatThreshold` is newly exported so the coupling between the two detectors is a named constant rather than a `5` repeated in two files. Closes [#702](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/702).

- **Anthropic's 1-hour cache breakpoint is now selectable, and its writes are priced at their own rate.** [#714](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/714) shipped prompt caching at the 5-minute TTL only, and said so in its own changelog entry: exposing the 1-hour knob would have understated every cached turn by 37.5%, because `pricing.Rates` carried a single write rate and a 1-hour write bills at **2×** base input against the 5-minute one's 1.25×. Both halves of that objection turned out to be answerable rather than permanent. LiteLLM publishes `cache_creation_input_token_cost_above_1hr`, so the rate exists in the catalog core-agent already regenerates from (17 Anthropic rows carry it, each exactly 2× that row's input rate). And the *response* says which TTL produced the writes — `usage.cache_creation.ephemeral_1h_input_tokens` / `ephemeral_5m_input_tokens` — so there is a right answer to bill rather than a guess about what the request asked for, which is what the original entry assumed was unavailable. `model.anthropic.prompt_cache.ttl` (`"5m"` default, or `"1h"`; anything else is a load-time error, since the two differ by 60% on write cost and an operator who typed `"1hr"` would otherwise discover the silent downgrade on the invoice) and `--prompt-cache-ttl` for a per-run override — the same checkout wants `5m` under a human at the keyboard and `1h` under a `--serve` daemon whose turns are minutes apart, which is the shape the gap was costing most: every 5-minute entry expires between turns, paying the write premium and never collecting the read discount. The flag reaches declarative subagents on the same path `--no-prompt-cache` already took, and the kill switch still outranks it. **The 1-hour count is modelled as a subset of the write bucket, not a fourth bucket**: `TurnUsage.CacheCreation1hInputTokens` is bounded by `CacheCreationInputTokens`, so every existing consumer of the total keeps reading the same number and only pricing splits. `Clamped()` bounds it last, after reads take their share and writes take the remainder, so a contradictory triple can only shrink the premium-rated buckets — an under-estimate, never a phantom charge. An unknown 1-hour rate falls back to the **5-minute** rate rather than to base input, the nearer neighbour being the better estimate for a catalog row missing one field. The split travels the digest chain [#771](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/771) just built — `SubtaskResult` → `mcp.LLMFallbackResult` → the `savings` sidecar → `DigestSavingsRecord.SubagentTurn()` — because a 1-hour daemon would otherwise underbill every wrapped MCP digest by 37.5% of its writes, which is precisely the defect #771 closed, pointing the same way. Two surfaces stayed source-compatible rather than widening: `pricing.Rates.CostUSDWithCacheWrites` and `compose.MaybeWirePromptCache` keep their signatures and delegate to new `CostUSDWithCacheTTLs` / `MaybeWirePromptCacheTTL` siblings, both packages being on the stability promise above. **`usage.Turn` and `Totals` are deliberately not widened**: cost is computed from the split at `AppendUsage` time and `RebuildTrackerFromEvents` re-derives the split from the event's `CustomMetadata`, so the ledger is already right, and adding a field no surface displays would ripple into the wire projections for nothing. Leaving the default in place keeps the request bytes **identical** to a pre-`ttl` build — the SDK's TTL field is `omitzero` and 5m is the API default, so the marker is unchanged and an upgrade does not orphan the cache entries the previous build wrote. ([#770](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/770))

- **The `alert` tool's three service templates ship, so an escalation reaches Slack, Discord or PagerDuty in that service's own format instead of a JSON blob some receiver has to translate.** `slack`, `discord` and `pagerduty_events_v2` had been declared in `pkg/config` and specified in `docs/alert-tool-design.md` since the tool landed, and **rejected at config load** with "designed but not yet implemented" — so the only way to page from a distroless daemon was to point a `generic` target at a receiver that fanned out, which is a service to deploy, monitor and explain in order to reformat a JSON body. All three now render, and the config layer's accept-list and the renderer's switch are pinned to each other by a test that ranges over the exported `config.AlertTemplates`: a template the validator admits and the renderer does not know would be a daemon that boots clean and fails at the moment the alert is fired. **`slack`** posts Block Kit — a `header` block carrying `[level] summary`, then `details` as two-column section fields, ten per section — with `text` set alongside `blocks`, because a blocks-only message previews on a phone as "This content can't be displayed". Detail keys and values are entity-escaped (`&`, `<`, `>`): they are model-supplied data, and an unescaped `<` opens a Slack link that swallows the rest of the value, so `<none>` — what kubectl prints for an empty field — would render as nothing at all; the header is left alone because `plain_text` is rendered literally and escaping it would show the entities. **`discord`** sends one embed, colour-coded by level, which is where colour belongs: an embed has a first-class `color`, whereas Slack colour needs the legacy `attachments` surface. A summary too long for the 256-character embed title is preserved in full in `description` rather than cut. **`pagerduty_events_v2`** sends an Events v2 enqueue, and its routing key travels in the **body and nowhere else** — `/v2/enqueue` never inspects `Authorization`, so this template suppresses the per-target auth header entirely rather than handing the same secret to whatever sits in front of the URL, mislabelled as a bearer token. The key is still read from `auth.bearer_env` rather than a `routing_key_env` field of its own, deliberately: reusing the existing auth surface is what makes an unset key drop the target at startup under the [#762](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/762) rule, and PagerDuty is the last destination that should be advertised to a model and then silently unable to page. It is now **required** at config load for that template, for the same reason it is required for `switchboard`. Two details are promoted to first-class Events v2 fields — `details.source` → `payload.source` (PagerDuty requires it; default `core-agent`) and `details.dedup_key` → the top-level `dedup_key` — as ordinary details, so the model needs no template-specific argument, and removed from `custom_details` once promoted. `level: "resolved"` becomes `event_action: "resolve"` and **requires `details.dedup_key`**: the tool keeps no state between calls (the design's cross-daemon-dedup non-goal), so the correlating key can only come from the caller, and omitting it is a tool error naming the field rather than PagerDuty's "Event object is invalid". Across all three, **every documented service limit is enforced at render time** — Slack's 150-character header, 2000-character field and 50-block ceiling, Discord's 25 fields and 6000 characters, PagerDuty's 1024-character summary and 255-character dedup key — counted in **characters, not bytes**, because a byte-wise cut both discards non-ASCII text that would have fit and can split a rune into invalid UTF-8. Exceeding any one of them is a rejection of the *whole* message, and a model writing a long one-line summary is not an edge case; when details are dropped to fit, the message says how many rather than truncating silently. Details render in sorted key order in every template, so two identical alerts produce identical bytes. The `generic` payload's omitted timestamp — flagged in the design doc as a Phase 2 decision — **stays omitted**: none of the three service formats wants one (Slack and Discord stamp the post; PagerDuty stamps the event and takes an explicit timestamp only for backfill), and the eventlog record for the tool call is still the authoritative time. ([#749](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/749))

- **New `switchboard` alert template: an escalation can now land in a chat thread the gateway owns, instead of only in a webhook nobody can reply to.** `renderTemplate` implemented exactly one shape — `{"level","summary","details"}` — and it is not the shape [switchboard](https://github.com/go-steer/switchboard)'s outbound message API accepts (`{"conversation","text"}`), so there was no configuration that got an agent's escalation into the Slack or Google Chat conversation the gateway already bridges. The three service templates declared beside `generic` were no help either at the time: `slack`/`discord`/`pagerduty_events_v2` were still rejected at load with "designed but not yet implemented" (they ship in the same release, below). `template: "switchboard"` renders `{"conversation", "text"}`, where `text` is CommonMark built from the existing `level`/`summary`/`details` — the level in bold, one bullet per detail with the key in backticks, **sorted**, so the same alert produces the same body every time and non-string values render as compact JSON. It is a **destination class, not a service format**, which is what separates it from the planned `slack` template rather than duplicating it: Block Kit at an Incoming Webhook is one platform and one wire format, fire-and-forget into a channel, whereas one `switchboard` template covers every platform the gateway bridges — present and future — because the gateway owns the per-platform translation, chunking and rendering. What routing through it buys is the difference between a notification and a conversation: the message lands in a thread that can be edited, appended to and rolled over as an incident develops, and a human can reply. The **session id rides an `X-Agent-Session` header**, so the gateway can bind the thread it creates back to the session that raised the alert and route a reply into it rather than into a fresh session with none of the incident context. A header rather than a body field for a concrete reason: switchboard's ingress decodes with `DisallowUnknownFields` — deliberate on its side — so an invented body field is a `400` from every deployed gateway, not a field it ignores, while an HTTP header is the part of a request defined to be ignorable when unknown; one binary therefore talks to a gateway that reads it and one that doesn't, with no version negotiation, and switchboard already takes caller metadata this way (`Idempotency-Key`). Only this template sends it — a session id is internal routing detail, and a third-party webhook has no reason to learn one. Two new load-time requirements, because neither can be supplied later and both otherwise fail at 3am: the new `alerts.targets[].conversation` field (the gateway's conversation key — a Slack channel ID, or `channel:thread_ts` to post into an existing thread), which is **required** by this template and **rejected** by every other so a field that would silently do nothing cannot be set, and which is validated for whitespace and control characters exactly as the gateway validates it; and `auth.bearer_env`, reusing the existing auth surface for switchboard's ingress token, required because that ingress refuses to *start* without a token and so a target lacking one can only ever `401`. Being a `*_env`, an unset token also drops the target at startup under the existing rule, which is the intended interaction: a chat escalation path that cannot authenticate is not advertised to the model at all. `conversation` is a literal rather than a `*_env` sibling because a conversation key is not a secret, unlike a Slack Incoming Webhook URL that carries its token in the path. This does **not** reopen the design's two-way-conversation non-goal: the tool still posts and returns a status code, and nothing in core-agent listens — naming the session is what lets the separate, already-existing inbound path (`POST /sessions/{sid}/inject`, gated on `SessionACL.Contributors` from [#797](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/797)) deliver the reply. ([#798](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/798))

- **`POST /inject` computed a `prompt_id` and threw it away, so an attach client could not key anything by turn.** The id is minted by `inbox.push` and published on the `inbox`/queued frame in the same breath, but `InjectAs` returned only `error` — so the handler that had it in hand a line earlier answered `{"injected", "session", "woke"}` and dropped it. The consequence lands on anything that *renders* a turn. A chat gateway posts a placeholder with a live clock while a turn runs and retires it when the answer arrives; ask a second question before the first has answered and the two cross, because neither the placeholder slot nor the answer carries a turn identity: Q1's answer retires Q2's placeholder, and Q2 runs to completion with no clock. Usage accounting has the same shape — `usage-update` deltas accumulate against "the current turn", which is only well-defined while turns don't overlap. Nothing already on the stream substitutes. `turn-complete.prompt_id` is the right handle but arrives at the **end**, by which point the client needed to have known since the start; the `inbox` frames carry it but have **no `seq`**, so a mapping rebuilt from them desynchronises across exactly the reconnect it most needs to survive, while everything else a client depends on across a resume is either seq-deduplicated or idempotent; and correlating "the queued frame that just fired" with "the inject I just sent" holds right up until two producers inject on one session, which is the case that needs it. Both deliveries report it, waking and `"wake": false` alike — a deferred message still drains into a turn, and a response shape that varies with a request flag is one clients read wrong. `POST /wake` reports it too when the call carried a `prompt`, since a wake with a prompt *is* an inject; its 200 body also gains a declared type (`attach.WakeResponse`) in place of the hand-rolled map literal, wire-identical and now pinned by a fixture. The field is **`omitempty`**, unlike `woke`, and for the opposite reason: `false` is an informative wake state, whereas there is no informative empty id — absent means the daemon could not name one, and a client has to handle that whatever the encoding. It is **not a turn id**, and the inbox's coalescing is deliberately unchanged: N messages queued between turns still drain as one `[Inbox]` block, `turn-complete` still names one of their ids, and a client whose id goes unmentioned should collapse its state onto the turn that was named rather than wait for one that will never arrive — the fan-in is not new, the ability to *see* it is. Library side: `Agent.InjectAsContextWithID` / `QueueAsContextWithID` and the optional `attach.IdentifyingInjector` / `IdentifyingDeferredInjector` — siblings and optional interfaces, not widened signatures, because `pkg/agent` is inside the stability promise above and `attach.Registrant` is exported API; a registrant without them queues exactly as before and the 200 simply omits the key. Detected separately per path rather than as one capability, so a host that can name ids on only one of them degrades on that path alone. Forwarded on `attachadapter.Adapter` as well as `*agent.Agent`, and compile-time asserted there: the adapter is what hosts register, so an unforwarded pair would omit `prompt_id` from every production response while every direct-agent test stayed green — the same trap [#698](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/698) documented on `QueueAsContext`. Protocol stays **1.10.0**, still unreleased; the v1 inject fixture is kept frozen as the no-id shape, since the capability is optional and its absence is a live case rather than a legacy one. ([#840](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/840))

- **`read_only: true` on an `mcp.json` server declares every tool it exposes read-only.** MCP has a per-tool `readOnlyHint` annotation, but ADK's adapter doesn't surface it, so every MCP tool landed on the fail-safe *mutating* side of the runtime's dispatch classifier — including a whole server mounted at a provider's read-only endpoint, where the operator knows better than the runtime can. Three consequences, all of which had to be worked around per-tool or not at all: `wait_and_verify` refused to poll it without a `tools.wait_and_verify.poll_allow` entry naming each tool; its calls serialized on the mutation lock instead of overlapping; and under `plan_mode: required` every call was denied until `record_plan` ran, including the research a plan is made of. That last one has no per-tool workaround at all — plan-first checks the *namespace*, `"mcp"`, and never sees which tool was called, so it cannot tell `get_pod` from `delete_pod` even in principle. One declaration in `mcp.json` now settles all three, and `poll_allow` narrows to the case it is actually good at: one read-only tool on a server that also mutates. Scope is deliberately tight — the classification relaxes plan-first and nothing else, so a read-only MCP tool in `ask` mode still prompts and a `deny` pattern still denies it; `read_only` is a dispatch class, not an allowlist. A server-declared per-tool hint still wins where one exists, in both directions, so this can't launder a tool that says it mutates. It is allowed to move plan-first at all because it is an *operator* assertion about an endpoint they chose — the same authority as the config that turned plan-first on — and not a claim the server made about itself; point it at a read/write URL and you have disabled a safety property by hand. Two latent defects surfaced on the way and are fixed here: `renamedTool.ReadOnlyHint` had a pointer receiver while `Tools()` returns values, so the `#460` forward was absent from the interface's method set and every classification silently missed it, and the digest wrap — the *default* path for every MCP server — had no `ReadOnlyHint` at all, so it dropped the class outright. Both failed invisibly: no compile error, no test failure, just a hint that never applied. New `permissions.Gate.CheckReadOnlyToolCall` is a sibling of `CheckToolCall`, not a signature change, so out-of-tree hosts keep compiling and a caller that doesn't know about dispatch classes keeps getting the conservative answer. ([#693](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/693))

- **The digest wrap now covers the survey-shaped built-ins: `read_many_files`, `grep`, `glob`, `list_dir`.** The wrap was MCP-only, and the asymmetry was invisible to the model: a `gke_get_k8s_resource` returning 8KB arrived as a digest plus a `call_id`, an equivalent `read_many_files` arrived as 8KB of inline text and stayed in context for every remaining turn. In the `kube-platform-native` UAT session `019ffbae` one `read_many_files {path: "/opt/platform-agent-native", pattern: "*"}` put 54,449 characters into the window at call 7 of 16 — an estimated 40% of that session's 365K billed input tokens, for a shape the model reaches for readily. The four now take the same path: over 8000 bytes the response is replaced by the same synthetic `{digest, raw_bytes, method, call_id, savings}` map the MCP wrap returns, the raw payload goes to the CCR store, and `retrieve_raw` undoes it. Identical wire contract by design — core-tui's per-tool savings chip and the session savings ledger both light up without learning that built-ins exist. Membership is *survey*: tools that answer "what is out there" over a set the model named loosely, where a summary is useful and narrowing is cheap. `read_file` is deliberately excluded — it is the narrowing move a survey digest points at, and it is what precedes an exact-match `edit_file`; so are `bash` (output the model needs verbatim, no structure to prune) and `json_query` (the model already narrowed). Two departures from the MCP wrap: responses under the threshold are returned unchanged rather than re-shaped, and a digest that came out no smaller than the payload it replaced is discarded in favour of the original. Governed by the existing `--no-mcp-digest` switch, which despite its name now turns off the whole digest layer — the digest is only safe while `retrieve_raw` can undo it, and the two are registered on the same condition. ([#706](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/706))

- **The 5-minute cap on `spawn_agent {wait: true}` is now a default instead of a ceiling: `--subagent-sync-wait` and `tools.spawn_agent.sync_wait_timeout`.** `defaultSyncWaitTimeout` was a const with a doc comment saying *"revisit if real workloads need a different bound"*, and one did: in the PR #622 live UAT a GKE diagnostic subagent ran 6m21s, the cap fired mid-investigation, and the parent redid the entire job — 91 turns, $1.31, ending in a watchdog halt. Deep cluster diagnostics against a remote API, with hundreds of milliseconds per call and large truncated payloads, are not an exotic workload. The cap is on the *wait*, not the subagent: past it the tool returns and the subagent keeps running, its result delivered later as a pushed report, so the setting trades parent latency against whether the parent sees the answer in the turn that asked for it. Flag beats config beats the unchanged 5m default; the resolved value feeds the daemon's own manager and every per-tenant manager a multi-session daemon stands up, so a tenant session can't drift from it. An explicit `"0s"` is honored as *no cap* rather than snapped back to the default — `background.WithSyncWaitTimeout(0)` already means "wait until the subagent's own budgets end it", and promoting it would make that unsayable. Negative and unparseable values fail startup with the flag or config field named, since silently falling back to 5m would hand the operator the exact behavior they were trying to change. ([#692](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/692))

- **Injecting a message always drove a turn, so a watcher's corroborating alerts each preempted the work they were corroborating; `POST /inject` now takes `"wake": false`.** The inbox has always been an accumulator — 256 messages, drop-oldest, and several queued between turns drain as one `[Inbox]` block — but nothing could reach that behavior, because every inject fired the wake signal unconditionally. A GKE session took two `family.member` blast-radius signals two minutes apart, both about an incident it had already resolved; each drove its own turn, the second landed while the agent was still churning on the first, and the run ended in a 22-call tool loop the watchdog had to halt. `{"wake": false}` queues without waking: the message is appended, published as the usual `inbox`/queued frame, and read by the next turn, but it pierces no sleep and un-parks no paused loop. The flag is a tristate `*bool` (the `auto_continue.enabled` precedent) so omitting it keeps every pre-1.10.0 client's behavior exactly, and the 200 gains a `woke` field on **both** paths — its absence means an older daemon, which always woke. There is deliberately **no promptness guarantee**: an autonomous loop reaches the message on its own sleep timer, an operator-driven session when the operator next says something, a parked one when it is resumed. If a message needs acting on, inject it normally. A registrant without the capability answers **501** rather than waking anyway, because a silent upgrade would hand back the exact preemption the caller asked to avoid. Library side: `Agent.QueueAsContext`, `attach.DeferredInjector`, and `attachclient.QueueContext`. Protocol stays **1.10.0**, still unreleased. ([#698](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/698))

- **An approval is the moment a human lets the agent do something the policy refused, and the log couldn't say who did it.** `POST /sessions/{sid}/perms/respond` read `{id, decision}` off the wire, handed the decision to the broker, and discarded everything about the caller — even though the request had already passed the auth middleware, so a verified identity was sitting in the request context. The approval log answered "a `bash` call was allowed at 14:02" and could not answer "by whom". That is tolerable for a local TTY, where the answerer is whoever is at the keyboard, and not tolerable for the deployment this endpoint exists for: a relay (chat gateway, web console behind SSO) answering on behalf of a named human, where the only record of the most privileged act in the system is the service account that carried the click. `permissions.Approval{Decision, By}` and the optional `permissions.AttributingPrompter` are the seam — an **optional interface**, not a changed method, because `permissions.Prompter` is in the stability promise above and every out-of-tree prompter must keep compiling; the gate type-asserts and falls back, so a plain prompter behaves exactly as it did. `ApprovalLog` gains `By`, `attach.ApprovalInfo` gains `by` on `GET /perms`, and `POST /perms/respond` answers with `approver`. The daemon fills it **itself**, from `auth.CallerFromContext` gated on the `whoami` source, and records **nothing** when the source is `anonymous` — the anonymous caller's identity is a literal placeholder string, and a placeholder in an audit line is indistinguishable from a real attribution, so empty is the honest answer and a client must render "who approved this" as unknown rather than assume the key is present. The request body accepts an `approver`, and it is **checked, never believed**: it must equal the verified caller (mismatch → 400, prompt left pending), and with nothing verified there is nothing to check it against (→ 400, with the error naming `X-Asserted-Caller` as the fix). Accepting-and-ignoring was rejected on the same grounds as #797's `owner` — `readJSON` doesn't reject unknown fields, so a security-relevant field left undeclared is silently dropped and a relay believes it attributed a decision it didn't; trusting it would let anything that can reach the endpoint sign someone else's name to an approval. Two call sites, not one: `checkControlPlaneWrite` prompts on its own elevated path that bypasses every auto-approval shortcut, and it guards the most audit-relevant write the gate sees, so leaving it anonymous would have been the worst possible miss. `permissions.Serialize` forwards the attributed form, because it is on the daemon's default path and a wrapper implementing only `Prompter` would fail the type assertion and land **every** approval in the log anonymous while every direct-prompter test still passed — there is a test that fails exactly that way with the forward removed. Protocol stays **1.10.0**, still unreleased: `approver` and `by` are additive `omitempty` fields on shapes that 1.10.0 is already the floor for, and the pre-existing `{"acknowledged":true}` body is unchanged. `internal/attachclient` needs no change — attribution is server-side, so its callers get it for free. `coretui.ApprovalLog` has no `By` field in the pinned v0.22.0, so neither TUI's approval history renders the approver yet — both adapters have the identity in hand and drop it at the `coretui.ApprovalLog{Tool, Key, Decision}` literal, which needs the field to exist upstream first ([core-tui#277](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/277)). ([#830](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/830))

- **A session's `Viewers` and `Contributors` were enforced everywhere and settable nowhere; the attach protocol goes 1.9.0 → 1.10.0 with `GET` + `PATCH /sessions/{sid}/acl` and an optional body on `POST /sessions`.** `auth.Authorize` has consulted all three ACL roles since multi-session shipped, `SessionACLRow` has persisted all three, the design doc's authorization matrix documents all three, and both reference pages describe contributors as the mechanism for a shared session. There was no request a client could make that put an identity into either list. `POST /sessions` stamped the caller as `Owner` and took no body; nothing else touched the ACL. So the only reachable ACL in a running daemon was owner-plus-admins, and the two-participant session the whole role model exists for could not be constructed. What made it cost more than a missing feature normally does is where the gap surfaces: an unauthorized caller gets **404, not 403** — deliberate anti-enumeration — so a second identity trying to reach the session got an answer indistinguishable from "that session does not exist", and the downstream client (a chat gateway routing an incident thread) classified it as terminal and stopped. A capability that is documented, enforced, persisted, and unreachable fails in the one shape that reads as a different bug entirely. Both halves of the issue ship together because the switchboard case needs both and they compose: a creator generally knows its *audience* at creation time (the on-call group for an incident), which is `POST /sessions {"contributors": [...]}`, and does not know the *individual* until it happens (the specific responder joining mid-incident), which is the `PATCH`. `PATCH` rather than `PUT`, with `*[]string` on the wire: an **omitted** list is left alone and `[]` clears it, because with plain slices `{"contributors": [...]}` would silently wipe the viewers — the same silent-failure class as the bug being fixed. Both verbs require `SessionAdmin`, the **read as hard as the write**: the ACL names the other people on a session, and a contributor enumerating their co-responders is a disclosure with no reason to happen — and gating the write there is what stops the first identity you add from adding everyone else. `owner` is accepted on both endpoints **only so it can be refused** with a 400 that says why. Ownership is not transferable here because the persisted owner index is what makes an idle session visible to its owner, so a transfer takes the session away from the losing side with no undo; the *current* owner round-trips fine, so a client that GETs-edits-PATCHes the whole document needn't strip the field, and on `POST /sessions` the same field is refused because a body that appeared to hand a new session to someone else would be a security-relevant lie. Identities are normalized wherever an ACL crosses a trust boundary (new `auth.SessionACL.Normalized()`): trimmed, empties dropped, duplicates removed, caller order preserved, freshly allocated. That is not cosmetic — matching is exact, so a pasted address with a trailing space yields an ACL that *reads* correct and denies anyway, and the denial comes back as that same 404. The read-modify-write happens **inside the registry lock** — the new `SessionRegistry.AmendACL` takes a mutator rather than a finished ACL — because a `PATCH` whose omitted lists mean "leave them" is by definition a function of the current ACL, and computing that in the handler would let two concurrent `PATCH`es (one setting viewers, one setting contributors) each carry the other's field forward from a snapshot taken before the other landed. One edit would vanish behind a `200`, and the thing that vanished is an authorization decision — the responder the operator just added gets the 404 again. Atomics don't help with that one; only doing the merge where the value is held does. Ownership is refused there too, against the live owner, as `ErrACLOwnerNotTransferable` → 400; `POST /sessions` keeps its own inline check because there is no current ACL to compare against yet. Persistence carries the existing row's `CreatedAt` / `LastTouchedAt` forward, because `SessionACLStore.Put` is a whole-row upsert that stamps `now()` over a zero value and an ACL edit would otherwise re-sort the operator's `GET /sessions` as though the session were new; `Title` is left zero, which `Put` already reads as "don't touch". A failed write is a **500 with the in-memory ACL rolled back**, mirroring `registerWithACL` — reporting 200 for an ACL that evaporates at the next restart is the worst of the three outcomes, since the caller stops retrying and the divergence stays invisible until a responder is silently 404'd by the resumed session. A legacy unowned session is amended in memory only, preserving the resolved "ACL row exists ⟺ session is resumable" invariant. The `POST /sessions` body is parsed **before** the factory runs, so a rejected one cannot leave a constructed agent and a live wake loop behind. No middleware changes were needed and none were made: `isWriteMethod` already covers `PATCH`, and `browserWriteGuard` already forces `Content-Type: application/json` on every write body or not, so existing body-less `POST /sessions` clients are unaffected — a test pins that. `internal/attachclient` is deliberately not extended: it is internal, serves the TUI and `call_peer`, and neither has ACL UX; the consumer here is an external HTTP client. New minor rather than a patch because new *endpoints* are what 1.5.0 established a minor for, and a pre-1.10.0 daemon answers both paths with the same 404 it gives an unauthorized caller — there is nothing to feature-detect but the negotiated version, which is what makes forgetting the bump silent and why a floor test asserts it. ([#797](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/797))

- **`GET /sessions/{sid}/subagents` said what each specialist *is* and never what it can *do*, though `Catalog()` already held the answer.** `attach.SubagentCatalogInfo` carried `Name` / `Description` / `Model` / `Root` / `Modes`, and the projection read those five fields off a `SubagentTemplate` that also holds `Tools []tool.Tool` and `Toolsets []tool.Toolset`, and off a predefined `Spec` that holds `Tools []string` — then dropped both on the floor. "Which tools does this specialist have?" is the question an operator asks immediately after "what specialists exist?": it is how you tell whether a `cluster` subagent can actually reach kubectl, or whether a rooted subagent picked up its own `mcp.json`. Neither `/agents` nor `/subagents` answered it, so [mast-web#44](https://github.com/go-steer/mast-web/issues/44) rewired `/specialists` onto `/subagents`, rendered name / description / model / modes, and stopped there rather than inventing a tool list. Entries now carry `tools`, sorted by name, in the same `ToolInfo` shape and `source` vocabulary [#767](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/767) just gave `/tools` — including MCP server attribution, so a detail view can say `gke_list_clusters` came from the `gke` server rather than from a tool name that happens to start with `gke_`. The issue left two questions open and both are answered by where the data lives. **Shape**: `[]ToolInfo`, not `[]string`, because a name-only list re-creates the prefix-splitting guesswork #767 removed one endpoint over, and the extra cost turned out to be zero once the second question was settled. **Toolsets**: snapshotted at registration, which the issue floated as "may be the better trade" — a `tool.Toolset` is opaque at projection time (enumerating it needs a stub `ReadonlyContext`, may reach a live MCP server from under `m.mu`, and still would not say which server a tool came from), whereas `cmd/core-agent`'s subagent builder knows all three facts already because it is the code that pairs servers with toolsets. So `resolveSubagentToolsets` now returns a classified snapshot alongside the toolsets, `SubagentTemplate` carries it as `ToolsetTools`, and `Catalog()` stays a pure in-memory projection: built-ins from the resolved instances it already holds, MCP and skill tools from the snapshot. The detail endpoint the issue offered as a fallback (`GET .../subagents/{name}`) is not needed. Predefined specs resolve their names against the manager's catalog for descriptions, exactly as `resolveTools` does at spawn time, and the two edge cases are treated as the opposites they are: an auto-wired name (`return_result`, `report_alert`, `schedule_next_turn`) is **dropped**, because the runtime registers the real implementation itself and listing it would attribute a runtime tool to this subagent's configuration; a name the catalog has never heard of is **kept**, undescribed, because it is a misconfiguration that will fail `Spawn` with `ErrUnknownTool` and hiding it leaves an operator reading a healthy roster next to a subagent that cannot start. Names dedupe first-writer-wins with the resolved instances authoritative, the same rule and the same reason as `AttachTools`. The field is omitted rather than emitted as `[]` for an ungranted subagent, since "no tools" and "tools not projected" are different facts — which is also why the attach protocol goes to **1.9.0**: a client has to distinguish "this daemon doesn't populate `tools`" from "this specialist reaches nothing", the same additive shape and the same required fallback as 1.6.0's `title`. The `/subagent` listing in the local TUI renders the grant as a per-source breakdown (`4 tools: 1 builtin, 2 mcp, 1 skill`) — a roster is the wrong place for twelve tool names, but a bare count would not settle whether the specialist reaches MCP at all — and says `no tools` out loud, because an empty grant and an unrendered one look identical otherwise. The model-facing `spawn_agent` roster block is deliberately left alone: it exists to make routing possible from descriptions, and per-subagent tool lists there would inflate every request's prompt to answer a question the model does not ask. ([#768](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/768))

- **Sessions name themselves, and the attach protocol goes 1.5.0 → 1.6.0.** A remote session picker listed `core-agent/01a01b64-d7eb-7d25-8486-3a18431867e8` three times and asked the operator to choose; core-tui has had the slot for a human label since [core-tui#163](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/163) and nothing on this side ever filled it, so `Display` carried the mechanical `app/sid`. `*agent.Agent` now derives a short title from the session's **first prompt** — once, never recomputed, because an operator picks a session by what they asked it to do and that doesn't change when the conversation moves on — and exposes it as `SessionTitle()` / `SetSessionTitle()`, with `WithTitleModel` and `WithoutSessionTitle` to configure it and `GenerateSessionTitle` for hosts that want to drive it explicitly (a backfill, a retitle action). `GET /sessions` rows gain an optional `title`, pinned by `rest-sessions-list-v2.json` with v1 kept frozen; `internal/coretuiremote` promotes it to the picker's primary line and moves `app/sid` down to the description, so the identity is still there to confirm a pick. Four decisions carry the change. Generation happens **only when a title model is wired**: the automatic path will not spend a parent-model call on a six-word label, so a provider with no cheap tier falls back to the head of the operator's own prompt and makes no call at all — an agent that quietly grows an extra LLM call per session on upgrade is a bill nobody agreed to, and `cmd/core-agent` makes that decision explicitly by resolving the small tier via `ResolveSmallModel` and declining when there isn't one. The call runs on `context.WithoutCancel` with its own 20s bound, because a title is worth having even when the turn that triggered it was cancelled half a second later, and the alternative is a session that is permanently nameless because someone hit ESC once. Persistence rides **eviction**, next to the `LastTouchedAt` write that was already there: that is the moment a session stops being answerable from the registrant and starts being answered from the ACL row, and an untitled eviction deliberately skips the write so it cannot blank a name an operator set by hand. And because GORM's `Save` is a whole-row upsert, `SessionACLStore.Put` now `Omit`s an empty `Title` — an ACL edit long after the title was written would otherwise erase it, which reads as the feature randomly failing. A hand-set title always beats an inferred one, including one that lands while a generation call is still in flight — and `POST /sessions/{sid}/title` is how an operator sets one, since a name you can see is wrong and cannot change is a worse deal than no name at all, which is what core-tui#163 asked for in the same breath as the feature. It takes `{"title": "..."}` with the key **required**: `""` clears the name and re-arms automatic generation, while an *omitted* key is a 400 rather than a clear, because the daemon does not reject unknown fields and a typo'd key would otherwise decode to the zero value and silently wipe a session's title behind a 200. The 200 reports the title as **stored** — read back through the new optional `attach.SessionTitleSetter` rather than echoed — since the host normalizes and caps it and the response is the client's only account of what the picker will show. It also carries `persisted`, which is not a success flag: it reports whether the name reached the durable row, and `false` is the *norm* for the single-session `--attach-listen` daemon, which has no ACL row to write. A store that was wired and failed gets `false` **with a `detail`**, still a 200, because the rename did take effect and rolling it back would discard a change that happened — the eviction write-through would try to persist it again anyway, so a rollback wouldn't even be the last word. That is a deliberate split from #797's `PATCH /acl`, which rolls back and 500s: an ACL that evaporates at the next restart silently un-authorizes a responder, whereas a title that does is a label reverting to the inferred one. `SessionWrite`, not `SessionAdmin` — a title is a display label, not an authorization fact, and a contributor can already `/inject`, which is a strictly larger power. A host with no title-setting capability answers **501**, which unlike the 404 that masks a denial is safe to feature-detect on, since reaching it means the caller was already authorized. `internal/attachclient` gains `SetSessionTitle` and the remote TUI a `/title` slash — where clearing is spelled `/title --clear` and a bare `/title` prints usage, because a slash whose bare form is destructive puts back one layer up exactly the hazard the required key removes at the API; the in-process TUI deliberately gets none, since it drives one session and has no picker to fix. Protocol stays **1.10.0**, still unreleased. Config: `agent.session_title` (default on). ([#808](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/808))

#### Documentation

- **The metrics pipeline had twenty instruments and no page; `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER` was the documented way to "turn on OTel", and it turns on no metrics at all.** ADK-go ships no `MeterProvider` (upstream TODO), so `pkg/telemetry.SetupMetrics` builds its own SDK provider outside ADK — which makes `otel.metrics.exporter` / `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER` a **second switch**, not a detail of the first. An operator who followed the OTel page got spans, saw no metrics, and had no page to be wrong on: the trace page carried a five-line interim bullet, `reference/configuration.md` listed the config keys with no indication of what they produce, and the instrument inventory existed only as `docs/metrics-design.md`, a design doc that has since drifted from the code it described (it names `gen_ai.client.operation.duration`, `core_agent.context.window_used` and `core_agent.context.window_size`, none of which are constructed anywhere, and omits several that are). New `concepts/metrics.md` is written from the source tree instead: every shipped instrument with its type, unit, attributes and attribute-value enums, grouped as cost/usage, latency, and subsystems. The **Prometheus** column is empirically captured rather than derived — a throwaway test stood up the real `SetupMetrics` Prometheus pipeline, registered every instrument with its real type and unit, scraped `/metrics`, and the output is what the table says. Deriving it from the naming rules would have been wrong in at least two places: the `USD` unit is appended verbatim, so `core_agent.session.cost_usd` scrapes as `core_agent_session_cost_usd_USD_total`, and `By` on `core_agent.digest.bytes_saved` produced no `_bytes` suffix at all. The caveats are the part a table can't carry and are stated rather than left to be re-found: tool duration includes lock and prompt wait, per-session series restart on evict-and-resume, `core_agent.agent.compactions` is a process counter and not the eventlog scan, async instruments cannot carry exemplars even though `AlwaysOnFilter` is installed, `mcp.server.status` never transitions after startup, and `session_labels: false` aggregates *before* observing — which drops `core_agent.session.duration` entirely and turns `priced` into an AND across sessions — rather than merely stripping a label. Nine PromQL samples and a fifteen-panel starter dashboard (`dev/grafana/core-agent-overview.json`) come with it, and `pkg/telemetry/dashboard_test.go` chains the three artifacts so a rename can't leave a silently-wrong table behind an empty panel: every metric a panel queries must appear on the page, and every metric the page names must exist as a string literal under `pkg/`. The `example-otel` GKE overlay now sets `OTEL_METRICS_EXPORTER=otlp` beside the traces var, with `monitoring.googleapis.com` and `roles/monitoring.metricWriter` threaded through both READMEs and `scripts/setup-wif.sh` (`roles/cloudtrace.user` does not cover metric writes). **Not verified live, and said so in three places rather than implied:** the OTLP-metrics-to-Cloud-Monitoring half of that overlay has not been run on a real cluster — the site page, the component README and the overlay README each carry the caveat and link [#554](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/554), which stays open for it. The trace page keeps one bullet pointing here, and its opening now says outright that enabling one pipeline does not enable the other. ([#554](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/554))

#### Other (Cleanup)

- **Deleting the one line that carries per-session permission isolation into every tool call broke exactly zero tests; it now breaks three.** In a multi-session daemon, MCP toolsets and the built-in tool registry are constructed **once at startup** against the daemon's template gate, and nothing rebinds them — so the only thing that routes a tenant's tool call to *that tenant's* mode, approvals, prompter and plan-first state is `agent.Run` stamping the session's sub-gate onto the turn context (`permissions.WithSessionGate`) and every `Gate.Check*` entry point reading it back off there (`resolveSessionGate`). That mechanism was correct, load-bearing, documented in three separate comments — and unpinned. Removing the stamp left the entire suite green. This was found by investigating [#825](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/825), which read the same code statically, saw tools closed over the daemon gate with no rebinding, and concluded a tenant's declarative subagent must be asking the wrong gate — predicting four symptoms: `plan_mode: "required"` permanently unsatisfiable (a tenant's `record_plan` marks its own sub-gate, never the template's), the mirror where the daemon's own plan unblocks every tenant at once, cross-tenant approval grants, and `ask`-mode prompts failing with `ErrNoPrompter` because a daemon's template gate has no prompter. **None of them reproduce**, and the issue is closed as invalid — but its four predictions turned out to be an exact description of what a *broken* chain produces, which is what makes them worth keeping as assertions. Three links are now covered, each verified by cutting it and watching the tests fail: `pkg/agent/session_gate_stamp_test.go` (Run stamps its gate; an agent with no gate leaves the context clean, so single-user hosts don't get a typed-nil to defend against), `pkg/compose/session_subagents_test.go` (`ReproduceAgent` hands the same derived sub-gate to `agent.New` and to the session's subagent factory, two sessions get two distinct gates, and neither gets the template itself), and `pkg/agent/background/session_gate_test.go` (the far end: a declarative subagent's startup-resolved tool, holding the template gate, consults the spawning session's sub-gate — because `Spawn` carries the turn context into the subagent goroutine with `context.WithoutCancel`, which drops cancellation and keeps values). Cutting *that* link reproduces all four of #825's symptoms verbatim, including the `plan-first mode requires record_plan` refusal text. The sharing rationale on `sessionBackgroundRecipe.templates` — the comment #825 was filed against, which justified sharing on "toolsets are stateless process-lifetime handles" and said nothing about the gate — now states the actual reason, and `docs/multi-session-design.md` explains why the spawn tools are the one thing that *did* need per-manager rebinding ([#738](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/738)): they hold a `*Manager`, and no context lookup can redirect a captured pointer. No production behaviour changes. ([#825](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/825))

- **Nothing ran the example programs, so `examples/parallel-spawn` sat exiting 1 while its README advertised "Exits 0".** `go build ./...` proves the examples *compile*, and compiling is not what an example is for. Every one of parallel-spawn's three spawns had been coming back `background: ad-hoc subagents are disabled` since ad-hoc spawns became opt-in, its own "expected 3 spawned subagents, got 0" check was failing, and the whole thing was found by hand during unrelated work ([#476](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/476)) rather than by a check. `dev/ci/presubmits/examples-smoke` builds the runnable set in one `go build` and runs each program with **no arguments and no credentials**, failing on any non-zero exit — about five seconds for nine examples, so it is a step in the existing required `test` job rather than a workflow with its own name for branch protection to learn. Provider keys are stripped from the child environment: every runnable example defaults to the scripted mock provider and opts into a real one behind a flag the runner never passes, and a gate whose answer depends on whose shell it runs in is not a gate. The runnable set is a **list, not a glob**, because the examples split three ways — runnable unattended; deliberately not runnable (`basic` and `with-tools` demonstrate the real-provider path, `streaming` takes its prompt as an argument, `attach-daemon` binds a listener and never returns, `bouncer-preflight` needs a key and has its own tests); and recipes, which are config and manifests with no Go in them and are covered by `examples/internal/recipecheck` and the GKE e2e workflows instead. A list alone would rot exactly the way the examples did, so `examples/internal/smokeset` pairs it with a test requiring every `examples/*/main.go` to appear in one column or the other: a new example is either run or excluded **with a written reason**, and there is no third state in which it is quietly uncovered — the same shape, and the same argument, as [#812](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/812)'s exhaustive capability lists. The runner also fails when a run leaves the worktree dirty, since an example that starts writing into the checkout is a defect an exit code does not catch. **What it deliberately does not claim:** that an example demonstrates what its README says. Only `parallel-spawn` and `compose-multi-session` assert on their own results today; the other seven exit non-zero only when an operation errors, so "ran to completion" is the whole guarantee. Making each example check its own output is worth doing per example and is not what shipped here, so `CONTRIBUTING.md` asks for it on new ones rather than the gate implying it. The one structural oddity is recorded where someone will hit it: the runner lives at `examples/internal/smokerun` rather than under `dev/` with the other check tools, because Go's internal rule refuses an import of `examples/internal/smokeset` from `dev/`, and moving the manifest out of `internal` to satisfy a convention would have turned an examples-only list into a package the rest of the repo can depend on. ([#852](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/852))

- **The core-tui capability-guard lists were exhaustive as of one version and nothing compared them to the core-tui we pin; there is now a gate that does.** [#810](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/810) pinned every implemented capability with a `var _ coretui.X = (*T)(nil)` line, which closes the bug class [#804](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/804) was filed for — core-tui adding a *method* to an interface a host names is now a build failure rather than a feature that silently stops appearing. It does not keep itself closed. The lists were a snapshot of v0.22.0, so core-tui adding a whole *new* interface would land in neither list, be implemented by nobody, and produce no error and no log, because core-tui feature-detects capabilities by type assertion on `Options.Agent`. That is the same silent omission as [#802](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/802) and [#803](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/803), one level up. `dev/tools/verify-coretui-guards` (tool in `dev/coretui-guard-check/`, delegator in `dev/ci/presubmits/`, run as a step of CI's existing required `test` job) enumerates the exported interfaces of `github.com/go-steer/core-tui/tui` **at the version `go.mod` resolves to** and requires both hosts to account for every one of them. The version is resolved through `go list -json`, never written down: a drift gate with a hardcoded version is an instance of the bug it exists to prevent. `--print` dumps the interface × adapter matrix, which is the table to read when picking up a bump. The design problem worth recording is that guards are machine-readable and *declines are English prose* — and the prose is what makes an absence a decision rather than an oversight, so deleting it to make the check easy would have thrown away the point of the exercise. Instead each decline now carries a `//coretui:declined <Interface>` directive that the bullet hangs off, and the gate requires the directive's following prose to name the same interface, so the two cannot be renamed apart and the head comments become load-bearing instead of advisory. Five further properties fall out of having a checker at all: a guard line deleted in a refactor reads as an unaccounted interface; a decline for something core-tui no longer exports is a stale-prose error; an interface both guarded and declined is a contradiction reported with both positions (the tidy-up [#811](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/811) had to remember by hand when `RemoteInterrupter` moved out of the declined section); a guard or directive parked outside the two registered files is an error naming where it belongs, which is what makes "the guard files are the complete list" a property rather than an aspiration; and each guard file's `//go:build` constraint is compared against the adapter it guards, since a widened tag drops the guards out of the default build as silently as the bug they catch. Enumeration is stdlib `go/parser` over the file list `go list` reports, not `x/tools`' `go/packages`: the roster is a set of plain type declarations that the AST answers exactly, and `x/tools` is not in `go.mod` today even as an indirect requirement, so the alternative was a new direct module dependency for a parse. The guard files are read **by path** rather than through the build system for the same reason: `cmd/core-agent/coretui_guards.go` is behind `//go:build !no_tui`, and anything package-shaped would see zero guards under `-tags no_tui` and report the entire roster as missing. Current state at core-tui v0.22.0: 21 exported interfaces, 30 guards and 12 declines across the two hosts, all accounted for. One thing it deliberately does not claim to catch: a decline whose *reason* has stopped being true while the interface still exists — that needs a type-check of the host, not a comparison of two lists. ([#812](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/812))

- **Every core-tui capability the two TUI hosts implement is now pinned by a `var _` assertion, collected into one auditable list per host.** core-tui feature-detects optional capabilities by type assertion on `Options.Agent` with no error and no log on failure, so a near-miss — right intent, wrong method name or wrong signature — is indistinguishable at runtime from a host that deliberately declines the capability: the feature never appears and nothing says why. What made that survivable for so long is the substitute the tree had been using: a doc comment reading `// X satisfies coretui.Y`, which looks to a reviewer exactly like a guarantee and is checked by nothing. Both known defects in this pattern ([#802](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/802), [#803](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/803)) carried one and survived multiple core-tui bumps and a round of review each — one of them naming a `coretui.Interruptible` that has never existed in any core-tui release. Those comments are **not** deleted here: they sit on the two methods #802 and #803 are actively rewriting, and 44 more are accurate descriptions of what a method is for. What changes is that they stop being the only claim — every one of them is now backed, or contradicted, by a line the compiler reads. Coverage goes from six scattered assertions (one of which only compiled under `go test`) to 28, in `cmd/core-agent/coretui_guards.go` (local `--tui`, behind the same `!no_tui` tag as the adapter it guards — a mismatched tag would have deleted the guards from the default build as silently as the bug they catch) and `internal/coretuiremote/guards.go` (attach mode). The audit that produced them found no third defect: an exhaustive pass over all 21 exported core-tui interfaces against both adapters reproduced exactly #802 and #803 as compile failures and nothing else. Two decisions make the list worth reading rather than just long. It is **exhaustive**, not just complete — every interface an adapter does *not* implement is named with the reason, because an unexplained absence is indistinguishable from an oversight, which is the same failure mode one level up; that is how `ModelSwapper` on the remote adapter reads as "the model is the daemon's choice, not the attached operator's" rather than as a gap. And each list has **one home**: the assertions that were sitting beside their methods moved in, since a set you have to grep for is not a set anyone audits. `UsageTracker` on the local side is the one capability supplied by a separate type (`*coreUsageBridge`, wired through `Options.UsageTracker` because the tracker outlives a `/model` swap) and it is in the list anyway, so the capability doesn't read as absent. ([#804](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/804))

- **A frozen lookout pin meant "exempt forever, silently"; it now means "exempt until a date somebody wrote down", and the weekly job says so out loud on every run.** [#787](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/787) let a recipe decline the weekly upstream bump by being named in `Tracked.Frozen` *and* carrying a `pin-frozen: <reason>` marker on each pin. What it did not give the freeze was an end. On a week where every live pin is current the job exits with `drift=false`, opens nothing and prints nothing anyone reads, while the frozen recipe falls another release behind — the same failure #787 was filed about, a pin nobody was told about, differing only in that somebody meant it once. Three changes close that. The marker now **requires** a `review: YYYY-MM-DD` clause and `imagepin.Sites` rejects one without a parseable date, so a dateless freeze fails on the pull request that introduces it rather than in a scheduled run months later; a malformed date is its own error class, because reporting a present-but-broken marker as an absent one sends the reader hunting for a comment sitting right in front of them. The job now writes a **job summary on every run**, clean weeks included, naming each frozen pin, how many releases behind it now sits, and its review date — visibility is the fix, and a report you only get on the weeks something else happened is not one. And a new `--check-freezes` mode answers `freeze-review=ok` / `freeze-review=overdue` on stdout, which the workflow escalates to a failed step. Four decisions are worth stating. The verdict rides **stdout, not the exit code**, for the same reason `--check` does: `go run` collapses every non-zero child status to 1, so the tool's invariant is that non-zero always means the tool itself broke. The lapse is deliberately **not folded into `drift`** — `drift=true` drives a *rewrite*, and a frozen pin is never rewritten, so folding them would send the job into a rewrite producing an empty diff and a pull request about nothing. The freeze step runs **last**, after the bump PR, so an overdue case study can never block the pins that do track upstream, and it fails rather than warns, because a warning on a scheduled run is read by nobody. Distance is **reported, never gated**: the summary says how far behind a frozen pin is, but picks no threshold, since a gate built on a number guessed today is a gate someone disables next quarter. The in-tree coherence test asserts every frozen site has a date and pointedly does *not* check whether it has passed — a per-PR test that goes red on a Tuesday for a tree nobody touched is a test people delete. `examples/kube-platform-agent`'s three markers gain `(review: 2027-02-01)`; no pin moves, so the frozen case study still demonstrates what it always did. ([#791](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/791))

## [2.9.0-dev.3] — 2026-08-19

### Breaking Changes

- **`POST /interrupt` now holds the agent by default, and the attach protocol goes 1.4.0 → 1.5.0.** An empty body is still valid and still cancels the in-flight turn — but the agent now *parks* afterwards instead of walking straight into the next turn, because "cancel this turn and let the scheduler start another one" was never what the stop button meant. Restore the old fire-and-forget behavior with `{"hold": false}`. The response body gains the pause state and the list of subagents still running, and against a pre-1.5.0 registrant a hold degrades rather than 501s: the turn is still cancelled and the response carries `X-Hold: unsupported` with `paused: false`, on the reasoning that a stop which half-lands beats no stop at all. `attachclient.InterruptResponse` stops being a hand-copied struct and becomes an alias of the server type — the copy is exactly how it silently missed every field this change added, so anyone who had reached into the local copy's fields gets them from the server declaration now. ([#794](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/794))

### Changes by Kind

#### Feature

- **The agent loop gets a pause gate: `Pause` / `Resume` / `ResumeWith` / `InterruptAndHold` / `PauseState` on `*agent.Agent`.** `/interrupt` cancelled the in-flight turn and let the agent walk back into the next one, which for an operator hitting stop reads as "nothing happened" — auto-continue re-drove the loop, a queued inbox message started a fresh turn, and there was no state in which the agent was deliberately held. Claude Code's ESC (stop, hold, ask what to do instead, then carry on) had no equivalent here. `awaitResume` now sits at the *top* of `Run`, so a parked agent blocks before it starts work rather than after. Four details are load-bearing: the pause reason is **first-cause-wins** and `Interrupted` upgrades only, so a plain `/pause` landing on an already-interrupted hold cannot downgrade "your turn was killed" to "the loop won't start"; interrupt is **repeatable**, because `cancelInFlight` is no longer nil'd on use and the generation-keyed `clearCancelInFlight` owns its lifetime; the audit order is fixed so the pending prompt is marked *before* the cancel, and the record of what was interrupted survives the interrupt; and auto-continue stands down while parked instead of racing the operator for the next turn. `pkg/attach` gains the `PauseEvent` / `PauseState*` / `ResumeMode*` declarations because the gate speaks them, but nothing advertised them in this change — `protocolVersion` stayed 1.4.0 and `pause` stayed out of `supportedEventTypes` until the emitter shipped. `docs/operator-interrupt-design.md` is the design of record for the whole train. ([#793](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/793))

- **Pause and resume over HTTP**, so an operator parking an agent from mast-web, a remote TUI, or `curl` drives the same state machine the library exposes. `POST /pause` parks *without* killing an in-flight turn ("stop after this one") — a running turn has no safe suspend point inside a model call, and reporting `paused` while tokens burn would be a lie. `POST /resume` takes `steer` / `continue` / `abandon`, and steer queues the new instruction under interrupt framing so the model knows its last turn was cancelled rather than silently redoing the abandoned work. `POST /agents/{name}/stop` stops one runaway subagent; interrupting the parent deliberately doesn't, since subagent runs aren't resumable, so killing them stays an explicit choice — which is why every `/interrupt` response now enumerates what's still running. `GET /status` reports `state="paused"` with `paused_since` / `pause_reason` / `interrupted`, and a `pause` SSE frame fires on every transition **from the agent rather than the handler**, so an in-process park reaches remote watchers too. Both endpoints are idempotent and report whether *this* call changed anything, so two operator surfaces racing the same click don't produce a spurious failure. Capabilities gains `features.pause`; `attachclient` grows `Pause` / `Resume` / `StopAgent`. ([#794](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/794))

- TUI: bump `core-tui` v0.20.0 → v0.22.0. Both releases shrink the exported surface (v0.21.0 unexports 27 incidental symbols and merges four capability interfaces into two; v0.22.0 removes 27 more), so most of the work was answering renames — but **only one of the three changes that mattered reached the compiler**, which is the argument for reading the removal lists rather than bumping until it builds. The loud one: `SubagentLister` + `SubagentEventReader` merged into `SubagentReporter`, breaking the two `var _` assertions that pin the capability; listing the roster and reading a name's turns are not things a host can plausibly do separately, so the merge is right and both assertions collapse into one apiece. The first silent one: `AsyncSlashProviderWithPreamble` folded into the bare `AsyncSlashProvider`, and nothing broke because both adapters already returned `(preamble, channel)` — but nothing *would* have, because neither adapter pinned the interface with a `var _`. core-tui discovers that path by type assertion, so a drift degrades to the synchronous `InvokeSlash`: still correct, and it freezes the TUI for the 1–10s a real `/btw` or `/compact` takes. Both adapters pin it now, the way `SubagentReporter` already was. `Options.Asker` is deliberately **not** wired — it is a feature (an ask-the-user tool with no tool behind it yet) and it wants its own change. ([#801](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/801))

#### Bug or Regression

- **`/btw` returned infra errors or nothing at all, and there were five distinct causes.** (1) The side question inherited the session's tool declarations and provider builtins, so a question could trigger a tool call the operator never asked for — and could seed a Vertex context cache from a request with no system instruction, poisoning every later turn. It now sends an explicit tool-less config and suppresses builtins and prompt caching via context markers (`models.WithoutBuiltins` / `WithoutPromptCache`), which keeps the retry-on-empty and empty-tail-detection wrappers that a `builtinsLLM.WithoutBuiltins()` unwrap would have dropped. (2) An empty answer came back as a 500, so "the model declined" and "the daemon is broken" rendered identically with no detail; an empty answer is now an *outcome* — `agent.SideQuestionEmptyError` carries the provider's own reason, the adapter restates it as `attach.SideQueryEmptyError` (`pkg/attach` can't import `pkg/agent`), the handler answers `200` + `{"empty":true,"detail":…}`, and both TUI surfaces render `SideQueryResponse.AnswerText()` so they can't drift on the wording. (3) The commonest blank of all — gemini's bare `STOP` with no parts — is classified by `wrapEmptyTailDetection` as an ERROR, so it bypassed the typed-empty path entirely and reached the operator as raw provider prose; `gemini.ErrEmptyResponse` now wraps a provider-agnostic `models.ErrEmptyResponse` and `AskSideQuestion` converts it, after recording the usage the call still cost. (4) The 30s RPC deadline applied to `/slash/*`, which block on a whole model call, so a side question over a long history routinely blew it and surfaced as `context deadline exceeded` — the "infra error" in the report; slash calls now get a 5-minute backstop via a second `http.Client`, and never less than an operator-configured timeout. (5) A 429 from the slash cost limiter was an opaque status error and is now a typed `RateLimitError` carrying `Retry-After`. The question also gets a short session-status preamble (state, model, turns, cost, inbox depth, running subagents) merged into its *own* content rather than sent as a second user-role message, which `pkg/models/anthropic` would have put on the wire as two unmerged consecutive user turns. ([#795](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/795))

- MCP: an elicitation request the TUI cannot render is answered with a protocol **decline** again, instead of failing the call. core-tui v0.21.0 split that case into `ErrElicitUnsupported` paired with a placeholder `ElicitActionCancel`, where v0.20.0 reported a plain `ElicitActionDecline` and a nil error. `coreMCPElicitor` already checked `err` before mapping the action, so it never forwarded the placeholder as an answer — but it forwarded the *error*, which turns what used to be a clean decline into a JSON-RPC failure on the calling server's goroutine. MCP has a word for "nobody is going to fill this in", and it is the same word `DeclineHandler` already returns for the neighbouring case of no interactive UI at all: `ErrElicitUnsupported` now maps to `decline`, and every other error — a cancelled context above all — still propagates. ([#801](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/801))

#### Other (Cleanup)

- pricing: regenerate `pkg/pricing/builtin.go` from LiteLLM (2026-08-19). One real rate change: **`gemini-3.6-flash` halves, $1.50/$7.50 → $0.75/$3.75 per MTok** (cached input $0.15 → $0.075), landing it on exactly the numbers `gemini-3.7-flash` already carries. The identical figures are the thing worth checking rather than waving through, and they are correct — Google moved 3.6-flash onto 3.7-flash's introductory rate on 2026-08-13, introductory through 2026-12-31 and reverting 2027-01-01, so this row is expected to move *back* in a regen early next year. Nothing else changed but the `UpdatedAt` stamps. ([#800](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/800))

## [2.9.0-dev.2] — 2026-08-17

### Changes by Kind

#### Feature

- TUI: bump `core-tui` v0.18.0 → v0.20.0 and wire `Options.ClipboardWriter` on both the in-process TUI and `core-agent-tui`. **The transcript takes the keyboard**: `tab` moves focus between composer and transcript, `↑`/`↓`/`k`/`j` move a per-item selection, `space` folds the selected item, `shift+←`/`shift+→` pan over content that doesn't wrap, `g`/`G` jump to the first/last item, and `y`/`c` copy the item (or just the code in it). Copies now go to the **host clipboard** as well as over OSC 52 — `SystemClipboardWriter()` resolves `pbcopy` / `wl-copy` / `xclip` / `xsel` / `clip.exe` at startup, which for `core-agent-tui` means the operator's own laptop even when the daemon is elsewhere; the footer drops the `· osc52` qualifier once a host write is confirmed, so "copied N lines" is an observation rather than a claim. **Host capability calls moved off the Bubble Tea event loop** (`SwitchModel`, `Reload`, pricing `Refresh`, `Sessions`, `Subagents`, `SlashCommands`), with bounded contexts where the signature takes one, and the `/model` `/switch` `/theme` pickers now snapshot their list once at open, filter as you type, and open on the row in use. One inline call survives upstream and is worth knowing while diagnosing a freeze: `/switch <id>` — the direct-jump form *with* an argument, not the bare picker — still enumerates sessions and switches on the event loop, which against an unreachable daemon means the peer fan-out blocks repaints and `Ctrl+C` until it times out ([core-tui#191](https://github.com/go-steer/core-tui/issues/191)). **Untrusted tool output is escaped before it reaches the terminal** — tool args, responses, file content and bash stdout/stderr take one `ansi.Strip` + `\xNN` pass, so a file full of raw escapes can no longer clear the screen or retitle the window. Markdown and syntax highlighting follow the active theme, modals fit a short terminal and scroll, and a resize no longer re-runs Glamour over the whole history. Alongside the bump, `coretuiremote`'s subagent roster is now stale-while-revalidate cached: core-tui folded `SubagentLister` into the once-a-second host snapshot, which turned an operator-initiated read into a standing 1 Hz uncached HTTP poll whose nil-on-error return made the sidebar claim "none running" on any dropped request; the cache keeps the last known roster on a transient error and refuses to serve a failed fetch as data, so a daemon that fails the very first request self-corrects in a quarter second rather than asserting an empty roster for the full TTL. ([#778](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/778))

- ci/examples: **the recipe's pin of the upstream watcher image is now watched by something other than a person reading a manifest.** It sat at lookout `v0.11.0` for ten upstream releases, straight through the breaking `k8s-event-watcher` → `lookout-watch` rename, and [#788](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/788) fixed that skew and added the in-tree consistency guards; this is the part that covers the next ten. A new `dev/lookout-pin-check` resolves `go-steer/k8s-lookout`'s latest published release, compares it against every place the tag is written down, and — in its default mode — **rewrites them**, so the weekly `.github/workflows/lookout-pin-check.yml` auto-PR carries a real diff and the recipe's kind e2e runs against the proposed image before anyone merges it. The generated body carries upstream's release notes for every intervening release plus a checklist naming the three things that have actually broken here before (renames, RBAC requirement changes, flag defaults), because a bump PR whose body is a version string sends the reviewer to another repo to do the work. Four decisions are worth stating. It is a **second gate, not an extension of the first**: [#680](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/680)'s `recipecheck` answers "can this daemon run this config?" offline in `test-unit` on every PR, this one has to call another repo, and merging them would have put a network call in the unit-test path — so the upstream resolver is an interface and every test here runs offline against a captured release list. The verdict rides on **stdout** (`drift=true` / `drift=false`), never the exit status, for the same reason the pricing regen does: `go run` collapses every non-zero child status to 1, so an unreachable API would otherwise be indistinguishable from a real release and the job would open a pull request on nothing. **Freezing takes two things that must agree** — the recipe is named in `Tracked.Frozen` in `internal/imagepin/lookout.go`, *and* each of its pins carries a `pin-frozen: <reason>` comment attached to the pin itself. The declaration is the exemption and the marker is the explanation, so a marker under a recipe nobody declared is a hard error rather than a quiet opt-out (a comment must never be able to exempt the pin below it on its own), and a declared recipe with no marker is a hard error too. Attachment is positional, and the split is by **site kind, not by file**: a kustomize `newTag:` is the one kind read off the YAML node tree, so its marker may be the head or line comment of the `images:` entry or of either of its keys; **every other kind** — an image reference (including a raw `image:` inside a kustomization's `patches:` block), a prose mention, a declared literal — takes the contiguous block of `#` lines immediately above the reference, where a blank line ends the block and a trailing comment on the reference's own line is not read. A marker also has to own its comment line and give a real reason, so a worked example indented inside a larger comment — or one whose reason is an angle-bracketed placeholder — is inert. `examples/kube-platform-agent` is declared and marked on all three of its sites ([#704](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/704)), and drift confined to a frozen recipe reports `drift=false`. And the rewrite **verifies its own work** by re-discovering every site in the tree it just wrote, checking the non-frozen count as well as the tag so an edit that makes a site undiscoverable cannot pass vacuously; if any single site fails, it restores the originals and exits non-zero, because a half-bumped tree — manifests moved, `DEMO.md`'s `crane digest` preflight not — is the exact shape of skew this issue is about.
