The pin walker itself is shared rather than duplicated: it moves to `internal/imagepin` (a module-root package, since `dev/` cannot import `examples/internal/...`) with the image family as a parameter, `recipecheck` passes the daemon family, and a differential driver run over both the old and the new gate produced byte-identical findings on the pre-#785 tree and on today's, and a second image family is now a declaration rather than a rewrite. The core-agent images are deliberately still out of scope. ([#787](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/787))

#### Bug or Regression

- examples/recipecheck: the executability gate now scans **subagent content roots**, and judges what it finds there against **that subagent's catalog**. Since [#619](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/619) a declarative subagent can carry its own `root` — its own `AGENTS.md`, `skills/` and `mcp.json` — and the checker walked only the recipe's own `skills/` plus one tree per `content_roots` entry. When `kube-platform-agent` moved its six GKE domain skills into `cluster/` so the `cluster` subagent could own them ([#621](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/621)), those skills stopped being checked by anything, silently: no test failed, the waived count just dropped and the waiver text went on claiming to cover both trees. Walking the tree is only half the fix — a rooted subagent *narrows* both reachability dimensions (built-ins by the `subagents[].tools` allowlist, MCP namespaces to the root's own `mcp.json` filtered by `subagents[].mcp`), so content there is now checked against the catalog it is actually loaded with; a skill naming a tool the parent has and the subagent does not is unreachable in the only place it is ever handed to a model, and checking it against the parent waved it through. Alongside it, a CLI name is a finding only in an **executable position**: inside a shell code fence, on a `$ ` transcript line, or in an inline code span that *invokes* the command — the command first, then at least one argument. The bare word match failed a skill for telling the truth about its runtime ("there is **no** `kubectl` and **no** `gcloud`" cost two findings), which taught precisely the wrong lesson, and `fleet-audit`'s schema vocabulary ("it must name one of `kubectl`, `gcloud`, `gsutil`, `bq`, `helm`, or `curl`") cost four more. The argv, not the fence, is what carries this: real runbook content puts its commands in numbered steps and table cells, and a fence-only rule scored **8** findings on the pre-[#644](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/644) `gke-troubleshoot-agent` tree the package was written for, against **81** before this change — it would have retired the check while leaving it green. With the span arm it scores **79** there, keeping all four `kubectl rollout undo` table cells; the two it gives up are argv-less imperatives ("degrade gracefully to `bash` + `kubectl`"), and that trade is documented in the package doc rather than papered over. Fence parsing was corrected while in there — a fence nested in a list item or a blockquote, and a closer shorter than its opener, were all mis-parsed. Net effect on the one waived recipe: `kube-platform-agent` goes from 141 waived findings (`upstream/skills/` alone) to 188 across both named trees — 120 upstream (21 prose mentions dropped) and 68 in the newly visible `cluster/skills/`, which nothing had walked since #621. Each waived tree now also carries a `WaiveMinFindings` floor, so a glob that stops matching anything fails loudly instead of reading as a clean tree — the failure mode this issue *is*. The underlying vendored content is untouched; that gap is now resolved as accept-and-disclose, documented in the recipe README ([#674](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/674)) ([#766](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/766))

- **Every deployable recipe pinned a daemon image that could not run its own config, and nothing noticed.** `gke-troubleshoot-agent`'s two overlays pinned `2.8.0` under a config rebuilt on v2.9's `alerts` and `tools.wait_and_verify`; `kube-platform-agent`'s two pinned `2.9.0`, a version this repo has never cut, so the Pod could not even pull; `gke-deploy`'s base carried an untraceable `main`-build digest under a comment promising to sweep it back to a release tag five releases ago; and `cloud-run-deploy` — which has no manifests at all and pins the daemon through its Dockerfile `FROM` and its deploy script's `IMAGE_REF` — sat on the rolling `:main` tag under the same expired promise, so two runs of that script a week apart deployed different daemons with no record of which. The first of those is the interesting one, and it is [#644](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/644) one layer down: `pkg/config` has no `DisallowUnknownFields`, so an older daemon handed a newer recipe's config does **not** fail — it boots clean, drops the blocks it doesn't know, registers neither `alert` nor `wait_and_verify`, and then runs a triage skill instructing the model to call them. There is no version error anywhere in that; the symptom is an agent that never reaches RESOLVED and never pages. All four overlays now pin `2.9.0-dev.1` (the newest published v2.9 image), `gke-deploy` and `cloud-run-deploy` pin the `2.8.0` GA (nothing in their configs needs v2.9), and both v2.9 recipes' READMEs, the runbook, and the site docs state the floor where a copier will read it. The durable half is a new check in `examples/internal/recipecheck`: a hand-maintained feature→minimum-version table is applied by reflection to each discovered recipe's parsed config, and the resulting floor is compared against the reference the recipe's deploy artifacts actually pin — a kustomize overlay's `images:` transformer, resolved through `resources:`/`components:` composition so an overlay that inherits a pin instead of restating it is judged on the pin it really gets, *and* a recipe Dockerfile's `FROM`, because a check that read only kustomizations would have called `cloud-run-deploy` clean. Three rules, with deliberately different preconditions: a parseable tag must name a version with a section in `CHANGELOG.md` (this is what catches a never-published `2.9.0`, which pure ordering waves through) and that rule is unconditional; a floating tag (`main`, `main-<sha>`, `latest`) or a bare digest is a *finding* rather than a silent pass, but only for a recipe whose config asserts a floor; and the pin must be at or above that floor. Digest pins keep an escape hatch — a `# core-agent-version: <release>` comment records what the digest is, because kustomize makes `digest:` and `newTag:` mutually exclusive and a gate that forbids the stronger pinning practice is a gate someone deletes. The table is the obvious weak point, so it is guarded twice: `RequiredVersion` errors on a path that no longer resolves against `pkg/config` (a renamed field can't leave a row quietly dead), and a golden fingerprint of the entire 147-path config schema fails the build on any newly added field with a message asking whether it is version-gated — which is the answer to "what happens when someone adds a third gated feature and forgets the table". ([#680](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/680))

- **`gke-troubleshoot-agent`'s watcher moves from lookout `v0.11.0` to `v0.21.0`, and its enrichment starts working for the first time.** Two things were wrong. The first is the pin: `v0.17.0` retired the `k8s-event-watcher` transition naming the recipe was born with, so the ServiceAccount, Deployment, ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding, the token Secret (`k8s-event-watcher-token` → `lookout-watch-token`), the Prometheus prefix (`k8s_event_watcher_*` → `lookout_*`) and the stderr log prefix all changed names at once. That makes the new pin a **floor, not a preference** — every manifest, the daemon's `proxy_identities` entry (`sa:k8s-event-watcher` → `sa:lookout-watch`, matching the same rename [#621](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/621) made in `kube-platform-agent`), and the e2e's metric assertion now speak the new names, so pinning back below `v0.17.0` breaks the recipe rather than merely aging it. `v0.11.0` remains the older, still-live floor underneath it (Content-Type on the bodyless `POST /sessions`, which [#383](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/383)'s CSRF guard rejects with 415 without it). The second is RBAC, and it is the one that was silently costing the agent evidence on every incident: the watcher's ClusterRole granted `events: list/watch` plus `pods: get`, and lookout's enrichment resolves an incident's pod through its owner chain with one namespace-scoped **list** pass — with `get` alone that pass listed zero objects, the target lookup failed, and every inject arrived carrying an `enrichment_error stage=resolve` trailer instead of the warm bundle the triage skill is written against (observed in a kind run as `enrich e2e-pipeline/broken-image-pod: 164B (outcome=failed, sections=0, errors=1)` — the same failure [#618](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/618) found in `kube-platform-agent`, and *not* covered by lookout#192's per-resource tolerance, which skips a Forbidden on an individual kind but still needs the target's own kind listable). The role now reads `pods/log` and grants `list` on **seventeen of the eighteen kinds** lookout's enrichment lists — pods, the workload kinds, and the reference kinds the bundle's edges section checks — and the same trigger now enriches to a ~2 KB bundle, `outcome=ok, sections=5, errors=0` (the exact byte count is in the e2e's own output, where it stays current). It stays `get`/`list`/`watch`-only, and the withheld set is now exactly one thing: `list` on **Secrets**, which returns secret values cluster-wide and is not a grant a propose-only demo recipe should ask an operator to accept (enrichment renders a secret-free partial with `skipped=secrets`, never a failure). Three kinds that the old role also withheld — nodes, endpointslices, serviceaccounts — are granted list-only here rather than documented as gaps: they are cheap metadata reads (node and endpoint topology is effectively public in-cluster, and `serviceaccounts` list returns names and secret *references*, never values), and withholding them only cost the bundle sections. One grant does carry real risk and the manifest header now says so instead of leaving it inside the "narrowing" framing: `get` on `pods/log`, cluster-wide, over logs that routinely carry credentials and PII. It is kept because the bundle's logs section is the evidence the triage skill reasons from, with a pointer to scoping the watcher to a namespaced Role for clusters that cannot accept it. The Deployment declares the one real gap up front with `--enrich-lists=all,-secrets` instead of letting each incident rediscover it through a rejected LIST, and a new `TestWatcherRBACMatchesEnrichLists` asserts the role's rules and that flag value against each other and against a copy of upstream's requirement list, so the two cannot drift apart silently; `--enrich-lists-preflight` is deliberately left unset, since subtracting the only denied list leaves its SelfSubjectAccessReview probe nothing to find. Also new: `TestWatcherImagePinIsConsistent`, which holds the base Deployment, both overlays' kustomize image transforms, and the e2e's `WATCHER_IMAGE` default to one tag — and `TestWatcherTagInDocsIsCurrent`, which extends that to the five documents that quote the tag to a reader. The docs half is not cosmetic: `DEMO.md`'s preflight *shells out* (`crane digest ghcr.io/go-steer/lookout:<tag>`), so a bump that misses it hands an operator a command verifying a tag the recipe no longer deploys. Each document must state the current tag at least once, so dropping the sentence fails rather than passing vacuously ([#674](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/674)'s `TestPublishedFindingCountsMatchTheDocs` is the precedent). The Deployment's `--sources=k8s-events` pin is what keeps the source set small, and both manifests now say so in those words: lookout constructs only the sources the set names, and an unnamed source is never built and reads nothing whatever RBAC allows — which is the load-bearing reason this RBAC posture is safe. Reasoning from the verbs instead is a trap the upstream tree disproves (`pkg/sources/expiry` declares secrets/serviceaccounts/webhook-configs `list` with no `watch`; `pkg/sources/saturation` declares nodes/pods `list`), so "we only granted list" would not by itself keep a source off. The pin's one behavioral effect today is dropping `ingress`, which rides the same events grant and would come on under `--sources=auto` — right for this recipe, since the triage skill ships no ingress reference and ingress events would make the e2e non-deterministic. `--storm=off` is pinned for intent only: the topology graph probes pods/nodes/replicasets list+watch, so `--storm=auto` already resolves off. There is no fail-fast argument for either pin, because a `k8s-events` miss is fatal under `auto` too, by upstream design. **Operators upgrading in place must re-apply the ClusterRole**, and should delete the old objects after cutover, since the renamed set is created alongside them rather than replacing them: the `k8s-event-watcher`-named ones, and — because the two cluster-scoped objects also pick up the `-gke-troubleshoot` suffix `kube-platform-agent` already uses, so that a narrowed copy of lookout's role cannot silently overwrite a coexisting lookout install's — any bare-named `clusterrole/lookout-watch` and `clusterrolebinding/lookout-watch` from an intermediate deploy. Two more upstream changes ride along without action: `v0.18.0` added a `cluster` label to every `lookout_*` series (harmless here — the recipe sets `--cluster-name`, and the e2e's assertions match on substrings), and `v0.21.0` fixed `--otel-exporter=otlp` building no exporter at all unless an endpoint env var was set, which is the shape `deploy/components/otel/` relies on — it sets `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER` and leaves the endpoint to GKE's `Instrumentation` CR, so off-GKE that combination previously logged a boot line claiming OTLP and exported nothing; spans also now default to `service.name=lookout` instead of the SDK's `unknown_service:` placeholder. `v0.21.0` also dropped lookout's own import of this module, which changes nothing on this side: the coupling was one-directional and nothing here has ever imported lookout. Verified by a green local kind run of `dev/tools/e2e-recipe-gke-troubleshoot-agent` against `v0.21.0` — worth recording that the trigger survived `v0.16.0`'s crash-loop debounce because a `Back-off pulling image` event canonicalizes to the image-pull family, which is gated separately and only when the pull failure is classified retryable. `kube-platform-agent` is deliberately **not** bumped: it is a frozen portability case study ([#704](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/704)) whose `v0.18.0` pin does not track latest, and its manifest and recipe test now say so where the next person will read them.

#### Documentation

- examples: `kube-platform-agent`'s README now states, in a `What does not execute` section a reader meets right after `What you get` and long before the deploy steps, that **the vendored skill content tells the agent to run `kubectl` and `gcloud` while the recipe has no shell**. The gap was not secret — `AGENTS.md`'s overlay says "no shell", and the `recipecheck` executability gate has waived it since [#645](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/645) — but nothing a reader of the recipe would meet stated its *size*: the README's only executability caveat covered the four skills carrying `scripts/*.py`, which reads as the full extent of the problem and is a small fraction of it. The numbers are now stated rather than gestured at: 188 findings, 120 in `upstream/skills/` and 68 in `cluster/skills/`, of which 183 are the shell gap (84 ` ```bash ` fences, 59 `kubectl`, 39 `gcloud`, 1 `curl`) and the remaining 5 are the checker's coarse rules firing on the English word "alert" in prose and on a `acme__fleet` GitOps directory name. So is the honest version of what the runtime does with those steps, which is *nothing automatic*: there is no translation layer, a `kubectl` line is markdown the model reads, and the recipe's overlays steer it toward the equivalent read-only `gke_*` call or a proposed command — the good case observed in live UAT, but not a guarantee, and the failure mode is a silent skip or a confabulated result rather than an error, because an uncalled tool throws nothing. **Treat the recipe's output as proposals to check, not as verified state.** [#674](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/674) is resolved as accept-and-disclose over its two alternatives, with the reasoning recorded in the README: enabling `bash` fixes nothing (the brain image is distroless, so a shell finds no `kubectl`/`gcloud` to run) and gives up the propose-only-*by-construction* property [#617](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/617)/[#621](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/621) built, trading a fact CI sees offline for a runtime "command not found"; and an `AGENTS.d/` translation overlay mapping each step onto a read-only `gke` MCP call reaches a minority of the steps — **40 of the 99 CLI findings** are mutations, executions, or interactive channels (`kubectl apply`/`exec`/`port-forward`, `gcloud container clusters update`, …) that a read-only endpoint cannot serve *by construction*, which needs no claim about its tool list, while whether it serves the other 59 reads is not decidable without a live dial. The README also records the two arguments **not** made, because this tree contradicts them: that an overlay is structurally too weak to override a skill (the CI-enforced [#703](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/703) fix is itself an overlay-precedence claim) and that an overlay is worthless for removing no static findings (so did #703's). The `recipecheck` waiver reason and comment cite that resolution instead of an open question, and a new `TestPublishedFindingCountsMatchTheDocs` re-derives all eleven published counts from the checker and asserts the README and the site page still quote them — the floors from [#766](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/766) catch a tree going dark, not a number going stale. Closes [#674](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/674).

- examples/gke-troubleshoot-agent: the recipe now says where its ConfigMap-based content delivery stops working. It ships its whole `.agents/` tree as one flat ConfigMap — correct at ~14 small files, and the reason it needs no image build — but keys cannot contain `/`, so every file costs both a generator entry and a hand-written projected-volume `items:` path, and the total is capped at 1 MiB. The README and the site reference now name both limits, note that the key-flattening one bites first while the size one fails the whole apply, and point at `kube-platform-agent` as the reference implementation of the OCI image-volume pattern for content past that crossover, including the trade it imposes (you own a content image: a build, a push, and a tag bump per change). This is one rollout item from that issue, which stays open for its remaining items. ([#611](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/611))

- docs: the successor design for `pkg/auth` now lives in the repo that will own the contract. `docs/multi-session-design.md` describes `bearer_table` — the only authenticator that shipped — as if a static token→identity table were the plan; it now carries a **Successor** note at the top pointing at [go-steer/purser](https://github.com/go-steer/purser)'s `docs/DESIGN.md`, which proposes extracting `pkg/auth` into a shared module and deriving identity from SPIFFE SVIDs, standard-CA client certificates, and OIDC instead. `docs/README.md`'s registry gains the same entry, marked as living out-of-tree the way `k8s-event-agent-design.md`'s cross-repo note already is. core-agent is one of five prospective consumers, so hosting that design here would have implied an ownership it does not have — and would have guaranteed the design and the API change it describes never landed in the same diff. Read it before changing `pkg/auth`. ([#789](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/789))

#### Other (Cleanup)

- models: `gemini-3.5-pro` is not a model, and two companion matchers had been carrying it as a needle that can never match. The 3.5 line went flash-first — LiteLLM's catalog publishes only `gemini-3.5-flash` and `gemini-3.5-flash-lite` under it — and the id reached the tree as taskclass's Gemini frontier default, was caught by mast's first live-credential run ([#530](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/530)), and was fixed there while the substring stayed behind in `modeltier.Classify`'s Gemini 3.x pro case and `usage.contextWindowSizeFor`'s pro fallback. Only the dead needle goes; `gemini-3-pro` and `gemini-3.1-pro` are real ids and stay. The three tests that pinned it move to inputs that exercise the same branch through a real shape — `gemini-3-pro-preview`, the one surviving needle with no coverage of its own, and `publishers/google/models/gemini-3-pro`, a Vertex publication name, which is what the fallback tier is *for* (a bare catalog id would start silently testing the other tier the moment a pricing regen picked it up). Both replacements were confirmed load-bearing by deleting the needle and watching them fail. No behavior change for any model that exists. The CHANGELOG record of #530 and the warning comment in `ModelForTier` keep the id on purpose — that is the history and the guardrail. ([#786](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/786))

- **The minimum Go version to build or `go install` `core-agent/v2` rises from 1.26.4 to 1.26.6.** `go.mod`'s `go` directive moved because `core-tui` v0.20.0 requires it — this is inherited, not chosen. `GOTOOLCHAIN` defaults to `auto`, so most installs simply download 1.26.6 and nothing is noticeable; a build pinned to `GOTOOLCHAIN=local` on a Go older than 1.26.6 now fails until the local toolchain is updated. Nothing else in the dependency graph moved. Side effect worth recording for anyone reading the release tooling: `go mod tidy` deleted the `toolchain go1.26.6` line, because it now only repeats the `go` directive. The toolchain we build and publish with is unchanged and still verified end to end — `resolve_toolchain` falls back to the `go` directive for exactly this shape, and a future CVE patch is ahead of the floor so the `toolchain` line comes back and takes precedence again. That fallback branch turned out never to have worked under `set -euo pipefail` (a non-matching `grep` aborted the function before it was reached); it is fixed and now covered by `verify-go-toolchain --self-test`, which runs as part of the pre-merge toolchain check. ([#778](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/778))

## [2.9.0-dev.1] — 2026-08-16

### Breaking Changes

- **The Gemini defaults move to `gemini-3.7-flash`.** The zero-config default (`config.DefaultConfig`, what a run with no `--model` and no `model.name` gets) and `taskclass.ModelForTier("gemini"|"vertex", frontier)` both change from `gemini-3.6-flash`. The `/model` picker's first entry follows, because entry 0 is what a reflexive `/model`+`enter` durably lands on; 3.6-flash stays pinned directly beneath it as the one-keystroke fallback. 3.7-flash is half the price of the model it replaces ($0.75/$3.75 per MTok against $1.50/$7.50) on the same 1M window and the same `frontier` compaction threshold, so cost ceilings and compaction triggers do not shift underneath a run. Mid and small are unchanged (`gemini-3.5-flash`, `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`). This promotion clears the last entry out of `deferredPromotions`, which had held it since the model tables were unified — [#579](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/579)/[#580](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/580) are the precedent for what an un-UAT'd frontier bump costs, and a live GKE UAT is still the gate this promotion is measured against. Pin the old behavior with `--model gemini-3.6-flash` or `model.name` in `.agents/config.json`; both ids remain priced, classified and selectable. `extras/scion`'s `extra-large` alias and the bundled examples follow the default.
- **The Anthropic defaults move to the Claude 5 line.** `anthropic.DefaultModel` — what an `anthropic` / `anthropic-vertex` run gets when `--model` and `model.name` are both empty — changes from `claude-opus-4-7` to `claude-opus-5`, and `taskclass.ModelForTier` follows for the two Anthropic providers: frontier `claude-opus-4-7` → `claude-opus-5`, mid `claude-sonnet-4-6` → `claude-sonnet-5`. Small stays `claude-haiku-4-5` (there is no Haiku 5). Rates move with the model — Sonnet 5 is cheaper than Sonnet 4.6 ($2/$10 per Mtok against $3/$15), Opus 5 matches Opus 4.7 — and both new defaults carry the same 1M window, so nothing about compaction thresholds or cost ceilings shifts underneath a run. The Gemini tier defaults moved separately — see the next entry; the mechanism that recorded that deferral as data (`deferredPromotions` in `pkg/taskclass`) is what forced it to be re-argued rather than silently extended. Pin the old behavior with `--model claude-opus-4-7` or `model.name` in `.agents/config.json`; both ids remain priced, classified and selectable.

- **`RunResult.FinalText` is now the last *substantive* turn's text, not the last turn's.** A turn that produced output but called no tool can no longer displace one that did, and the rule is carried across `Resume` in a new `final_text_substantive` checkpoint field (absent in older checkpoints, which decode to `false` — the permissive direction). `FinalText` is the fallback return value wherever `DoneDetail` is absent — a budget cap, a watchdog halt, a provider failure — so overwriting it every turn meant the caller reliably received the *worst* thing the run said: substantive turns come early, trailing turns narrate having nothing left to do. In the 2026-08-13 GKE UAT the root cause and manifest patch landed on turn 3 and the returned `FinalText` was "standing by in a healthy, inactive state". A run that never calls a tool is unaffected — it keeps last-wins, since for a pure-reasoning loop the newest text really is the best one. Embedders who genuinely want "whatever it said last" no longer have a field for it; ask for the session's final event instead. [#730](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/730) does not cover this: it removes the re-drive for bounded delegations only, while standing workers keep the loop and every abnormal-termination path still falls back to `FinalText`. Closes [#731](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/731).

- **A spawned subagent now stops when it stops working.** A background subagent with a terminal deliverable — the default for every spawn without a scheduler — ends at the first turn whose last model response asks for no tool, returning that turn's text. It still carries the `return_result` tool and its `report_done` / `report_completed` / `mark_task_done` aliases, ranked **above** the natural end: a model that calls one hands back a curated result and ends there, a model that simply stops ends on the natural rule with its last message, and neither can hang the run ([#745](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/745) — an earlier cut of this change dropped the tool from bounded runs, and since bounded is the default it left the alias net covering only the path models rarely take). Previously every spawn ran the standing-worker loop: `agent.Run` already terminates when the model stops calling tools, and the driver restarted it with `"continue"` up to the turn cap, overwriting the returned text each time. In the 2026-08-13 GKE UAT a triage subagent produced a correct root cause and manifest patch on turn 1, then spent six re-driven turns scope-creeping across four namespaces and returned "standing by in a healthy, inactive state" to its parent — $1.05 for a worse answer than the $0.10 it had already computed. A subagent that genuinely watches something keeps the old loop: it is selected by having a scheduler (`scheduler: "sleep"` / `"exit_on_defer"`, or a manager-level default), or forced with the new `Spec.Mode` / `SubagentTemplate.Mode` (`background.ModeStanding`). Embedders driving `autonomous.Run` directly are unaffected unless they opt in with the new `WithStopOnNaturalEnd()`. Because a bounded delegation is no longer re-driven, one that runs out of room returns a partial — so `spawn_agent`'s result and the pushed report now carry a machine-readable `stop_reason` (`natural` / `max_steps` / `budget` / `deferred` / `stopped` / `error`) for the parent to branch on rather than inferring from prose. Closes [#730](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/730).

- **Unattended runs now boot with active runaway backstops.** When neither the CLI flag nor the config field is set, a run whose stdin isn't a TTY — `-p` one-shot, a `--no-repl` daemon, a piped invocation — resolves `--watchdog` to `enforce` (was: `warn`) and `agent.max_session_cost_usd` to `$10.00` (was: disabled). Interactive REPL/TUI runs are unchanged: `warn` and no ceiling. The previous defaults meant an out-of-box unattended daemon had **no active in-session halt** — the #623–#627 guardrail machinery was wired but inert exactly where it mattered, since nobody reads a pod log's warn-mode alert in time. To restore the old posture explicitly, pass `--watchdog=warn` and `--max-session-cost-usd=0` (or set `safety.watchdog` / `agent.max_session_cost_usd: 0` in config); an explicit `0` now beats the default rather than reading as "unset". `--watchdog`'s flag default changed from `"warn"` to `""` (the unset sentinel) so the new config field can win — a script passing `--watchdog=warn` explicitly is unaffected. Closes [#642](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/642).

- **`bash grep` / `bash find` are refused by default.** A `bash` call whose command is search-shaped — verb `grep`/`egrep`/`fgrep`/`rgrep`/`rg`/`ag`/`ack` (→ the native `grep` tool) or `find`/`fd` (→ `glob`), including via an absolute path — now fails with an error naming the tool to use instead, where before it ran. Piped searches (`go test ./... | grep -v ok`) and `find` with an action predicate (`-delete`, `-exec`, …) are unaffected, since the native tools can't replace them. Set `safety.bash_search_gate: "warn"` (runs the command, attaches the advice as a `notice` on the result) or `"allow"` (disables the check), or pass `--bash-search-gate`. See the Feature entry below for why the tool descriptions weren't enough. Closes [#158](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/158).

- **`--task=debug`, `--task=research` and `--task=review` now change the tool set, not just the model.** Those three classes register the built-in suite **minus `bash`** and turn plan-first gating on (`permissions.require_plan_artifact`), where before they only tuned the model, the compaction threshold and the ask posture. An existing invocation that used one of these classes and expected a shell will get `tool 'bash' not found`, and its first mutating call will be denied until the model calls `record_plan`. Restore the old behavior per-knob with `--enable-tools=bash` and `--plan-mode=off` (both new), or drop the `--task` flag. `--task=implement` and `--task=chat` are unchanged. Closes [#160](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/160).

### Changes by Kind

#### Removed
- background subagents: the `list_agents` and `check_agent` **poll** tools are removed as model-facing tools — the background-subagent model surface is now just `spawn_agent` + `stop_agent` (4 → 2). A completed subagent already **pushes** its result back to the parent (the `[Background reports]` block prepended to the parent's next turn), and `spawn_agent { wait: true }` covers the block-on-a-result case, so a poll loop was redundant — and, being read-only and idempotent, was exactly the shape that let an interrupted call get re-driven into a runaway loop (the guardrails-train motivation). Operators still inspect *live* instances out-of-band via the attach hub (`GET .../agents`) or the TUI; `Manager.List`/`Get` stay for those surfaces and for `stop_agent`. `NewSpawnTools(mgr)` now returns the two-tool slice; `--no-background-agents` still disables the whole family. Any config `permissions.allow` entries for `list_agents:*` / `check_agent:*` are now dead (harmless) and can be dropped. A consolidated operator-facing status read is deferred (see `docs/unified-subagent-invocation-design.md` §D1). Closes [#625](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/625).

#### Feature
- models/anthropic: **prompt caching is now actually wired, and on by default** on both `anthropic` and `anthropic-vertex`. It existed only as `anthropic.WithCacheSystem(true)` — a library-only option no wiring path ever called, which even when set marked just the last *system* block. The system prefix is a few thousand tokens and doesn't grow; the transcript is the part that gets expensive, and the ADK runner replays all of it on every turn, so a session's cumulative input cost grew with the square of its length. Requests now carry up to Anthropic's maximum of 4 `cache_control` breakpoints: one on the system prefix (which covers the tool schemas too, since the render order is tools → system → messages, and which keeps paying off across *sessions* because it's identical for every session on the same build), and the rest rolling over the conversation — placed on the last cacheable block and then every 16 blocks walking **backward** from the end. The direction is the whole point: marking from the front pins the cached prefix at a fixed early position and re-bills the growing tail forever, which is precisely what [#714](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/714) measured against the Vertex context cache (cached prefix frozen at 15,192 tokens while input grew to 58K). The intermediate markers are not redundancy — a breakpoint searches back at most 20 content blocks for an entry to read, one agentic step can append more than that when the model fans out parallel tool calls, and a lone end-marker that can't see the previous turn's entry misses the read and re-writes the whole prefix at the 1.25× premium, worse than not caching at all; the extra markers are close to free because writes bill for the tokens beyond the longest existing entry regardless of how many entries get created. `thinking`/`redacted_thinking` blocks have no `cache_control` field, so they're skipped as marker sites but still counted toward the stride, since the API's lookback counts every block. Off switches at three layers: `model.anthropic.prompt_cache.enabled` in config, a new `--no-prompt-cache` CLI flag (deliberately *not* a broadening of `--no-context-cache`, which controls a Vertex `CachedContent` resource with a TTL and a create/delete lifecycle that has no Anthropic counterpart — one flag over both would misdescribe whichever provider the operator is actually running), and a per-request `models.WithoutPromptCache(ctx)` hint. That last one is set by four call shapes: the compaction summarizer and the checkpointer (own system instruction, no tools), the `/btw` side question (neither), and any `RunSubtask` running on a budget of two turns or fewer — the `agentic_read_file`/`agentic_grep`/`agentic_fetch_url` wrappers and the MCP LLM-digest fallback, whose session IDs are invocation-unique and whose terminal turn writes a large `tool_result` for a read that never comes. One `model.LLM` serves both the agentic loop and those one-shots, and their prefixes diverge from the loop's at the first block, so an entry written for them is a pure 25% surcharge on a call that already re-sends the whole conversation. Subtasks with a longer budget replay their prefix enough times to pay the write back and keep caching. The hint lives in `pkg/models` so `pkg/agent` needn't import a backend, and can only turn caching *off*. Subagents are covered on both paths: one with its own `model` resolves its own provider through `models.Resolve`, which never sees CLI flags, so the kill switch is threaded to it explicitly — and since overwriting `cfg.Model` also drops the parent's `prompt_cache` (it hangs off `model.anthropic`), the parent's value is inherited unless the subagent states its own. Vertex gets identical treatment because explicit prompt caching *is* available there — only *automatic* caching is first-party-only — and the breakpoints ride the ordinary request with no resource to create. Markers use the default 5-minute TTL; the 1-hour one is deliberately not exposed, since it bills writes at 2× where the 5-minute bills 1.25×, the response doesn't say which TTL produced a given `cache_creation_input_tokens` count, and `pricing.Rates` carries a single write rate — shipping the knob first would understate every cached turn by 37.5%, reproducing the exact defect [#263](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/263) had just fixed ([#770](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/770) tracks the second rate, which matters most for a long-lived `--serve` daemon whose turns are minutes apart). No minimum-cacheable-prefix machinery either: below the model's threshold the marker is ignored with no error and no cost, so predicting it would buy a model-version table to maintain and nothing measurable. `anthropic.WithCacheSystem` is deprecated in favour of `WithPromptCache` and deliberately keeps its original all-or-nothing meaning — it replaces the policy rather than editing one field, so a library consumer who wrote `WithCacheSystem(false)` to avoid the write premium keeps that outcome instead of silently acquiring the new history markers. Two placement details worth knowing: the marker chain tolerates a turn that appends up to 52 content blocks (about a 26-wide parallel tool fan-out) before the message cache misses for one turn and re-warms on the next, and a `pause_turn` continuation clears and re-places the markers on the grown request, since re-applying without clearing would stack a fifth marker and earn a 400. Design notes, including the prefix-stability audit — MCP `tools/list` is re-issued every turn and its order comes from the server, which is the one live invalidator — are in `docs/anthropic-prompt-caching-design.md`. Closes [#714](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/714).
- attach: the hub's peer registry can now be made **durable across restarts**, via the new `--attach-peer-state-file` / `attach.peer_state_file`. The registry was in-memory only, so a hub restart — a pod roll, an OOM kill, a config reload — dropped every registration at once and each peer stayed invisible until its own next heartbeat failed and it re-registered. Peers recover on their own, so this was never a correctness bug; it was a 20–60s window in which `GET /peers` answered the fleet question *wrong* rather than slowly, which is the worse failure for a coordinator deciding where to route work. The file is a JSONL snapshot rewritten on every register / heartbeat / deregister / prune via temp+rename (with an `fsync` before the rename, so a crash can't leave a valid-looking empty file where the old contents were), and reloaded at startup. Heartbeats are persisted, not just registrations — reload drops leases that expired while the hub was down, so a file recording only original registration times would discard exactly the peers that had been alive longest, and resurrecting a dead peer for the few seconds until the prune loop notices is a worse answer than omitting it. Two properties are enforced rather than assumed: the on-disk record is deliberately **not** the `Peer` wire struct, because `Peer.Owner` is `json:"-"` — hub-side authorization state that discovery responses must not leak — and persisting the wire shape would have reloaded every registration as *ownerless*, collapsing `canManage` to `admin || identity == ""` and silently undoing the [#384](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/384) enumerate-then-delete hardening on every restart; and the file is written `0600`, since it holds registration IDs and a registration ID is the capability that authenticates `DELETE /peers/{id}`. Records are re-validated on the way in (the file is operator-writable and outlives any one binary, so a hand-edited `javascript:` endpoint must not reach a TUI that will dial it with operator credentials). It fails loudly by design: a state file that exists but can't be read, a directory that can't be written, or the flag set without `--attach-peer-hub` all fail startup rather than quietly running in-memory while configured for durability — individual malformed *lines* being the one exception, skipped with a warning because those peers re-register within a heartbeat and refusing to boot would trade a recoverable degradation for an outage. New `attach.NewPeerRegistryWithState`; the default in-memory registry touches no filesystem. Part of [#595](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/595).
- tools/attach: new `call_peer` built-in makes a registered fleet **reachable from a turn**, not just visible. `POST /peers` and `GET /peers` have exposed the roster since v2.7 and [#595](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/595)'s state file made it survive a restart, but nothing in the model's catalog could act on it — a hub could enumerate six peers and still had no way to ask one of them a question, so multi-agent topologies degraded to an operator hand-carrying answers between daemons. The tool takes a peer **name** and a prompt, opens a fresh session on that peer over `attachclient`, streams its turn, and returns the answer with the peer's `session_id`. Enable it under the new `tools.call_peer` block; it is off by default and refuses to boot outside a hub — `enabled: true` without attach mode *and* `--attach-peer-hub` is a startup error rather than a tool that registers and then fails every call, since a catalog entry that can never succeed is the unenforced-claim pattern this milestone exists to remove. Four properties hold by construction rather than by instruction: the model **cannot name a destination** (there is no URL parameter to inject into — names resolve against the live registry only, so a prompt-injected cloud-metadata address resolves to nothing and the error just lists the registered peers, the same shape as the `alert` tool's named channels); **no credential is model-visible** (the bearer token comes from `token_env` in the hub's environment, never entering the schema, the arguments, or the transcript — and configured-but-unset fails the call instead of quietly going out anonymous); **every call is bounded** by an operator-set deadline spanning session creation through turn end and a response-byte cap after which the tool stops reading and flags `truncated`, so a peer that streams forever costs the caller its timeout rather than its context window; and delegation is **gated per peer** on `call_peer:<name>`, a key that moves with a `name` override and that declarative subagents inherit through the parent's already-gated catalog. Each call opens a **fresh session** on the peer instead of injecting into an existing one, so concurrent callers can't interleave prompts into a single transcript and the reply is unambiguously the answer to this request — which makes `attach.multi_session.enabled` a hard requirement on the *peer*, and a peer without it answers `501` with that fix appended to the error. The event stream is subscribed **before** the inject, because `turn-complete` is a live typed frame that is not replayed from the log and a stream opened afterwards can miss the turn end entirely. Peers whose lease has lapsed are filtered at resolve time rather than trusting the registry's 5s prune tick, so a call doesn't burn its whole deadline dialing a pod that is already gone, and a `turn-error` from the peer is surfaced with its kind and message intact rather than flattened into "the call failed". New `pkg/tools/peer` and `compose.BuildCallPeerTool`. Closes [#595](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/595).
- watchdog/agent: the behavioral watchdog now observes tool **outcomes**, and a new Warn-level `tool-failure-streak` signal fires when three tool calls in a row all return errors with none succeeding in between. Every signal before this read tool *calls*, which cannot see the failure in [#639](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/639): during the [PR #622](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/622) GKE UAT an agent that could not reach its cluster at all reported the incident "fully resolved ... everything is in tip-top shape" with no call having verified anything. The calls looked ordinary; the results were the story. What the signal detects is deliberately narrow and objective — a run of errored calls — and no prose is inspected, because a detector that tried to recognize an over-confident *claim* would be a heuristic about English wearing the costume of a runtime guarantee, which is the defect class this release exists to remove. It therefore closes the **evidence** half of #639 and not the honesty half: a model that has been failing every call is told, on its own next turn under `--watchdog=feedback`, that it has verified nothing and must report what it could not check rather than describe a system as fixed. It cannot detect a confident conclusion drawn from tools that all succeeded and said nothing useful. Severity is Warn rather than Critical by decision: under `--watchdog=enforce` — the unattended default since [#642](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/642) — a Critical alert halts the agent, and halting three denials into a legitimate RBAC probe would make the backstop the outage; runaway *behavior* stays Critical via the loop detectors. One success resets the run and re-arms the alert, and one alert fires per streak rather than one per failure past the threshold (under feedback a re-emitting signal is a prompt leak). Success and failure are read from ADK's reserved `error` key in the function response and flattened at the agent bridge, so the watchdog never sees a provider's response shape, and an unrecognized error *shape* still counts as a failure rather than being silently dropped. Result observation is an **optional** extension (`watchdog.ToolResultObserver`, `watchdog.SignalResultObserver`) rather than a widening of the `Watchdog` interface — that interface is documented as a plug-in point, and a third-party watchdog should not break at a minor version to gain a signal it may not want. Response parts are deduped against the same per-turn set as calls, under a separate key space, since the streaming aggregator re-emits both and a double-counted failure would trip the signal at half its threshold. Part of [#639](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/639).
- attach/eventlog: a subagent's inner turns are now **readable by the operator**, via the new `GET /sessions/{sid}/agents/{name}/events`. They were always durable — every runner (sync subagent tool, `spawn_agent`, `RunSubtask`, remote) writes through the *parent's* `session.Service`, wrapped so each event carries a `Branch` label, into a derived `"<parent>:sub:<branch>"` session row. Nothing could read them back. `GET .../agents` reports status and the last report; the `/events` SSE stream is scoped to the parent session row alone and stays that way deliberately, since widening it would push subagent chatter into every attached chat view. So during the [#622](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/622) UAT, a parent looping into a subagent was a black box from the outside — running, accruing cost, for reasons only recoverable by capturing the raw event stream. The endpoint answers the question directly: turns in `{seq, event}` form, `?since=` / `?limit=` paging (default 500, capped 5000) with `next_since` + `truncated`. It queries the **log, not the live manager**, which is what makes it useful — a subagent that has already finished, or that ran before the last daemon restart, reads back the same as one still running. One name is matched across all four branch spellings the launch paths use (`<name>`, `bg.<name>`, `sub.<name>`, `remote.<name>`), each covering its own nested descendants: a declarative subagent is reachable *both* as a parent tool and by reference through `spawn_agent` ([#626](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/626)), so querying a single prefix would have returned nothing for whichever half the operator didn't guess. An unrecognised name is an empty list rather than **404** — "never spawned" and "finished three restarts ago" are indistinguishable from the log, and 404 would be a lie for the second — with the searched branch list echoed back so an empty result is legible. New `eventlog.WithAnyBranchPrefix` generalizes `WithBranchPrefix` (which now delegates to it, so repeated calls OR rather than last-one-wins), and the branch filter now **escapes LIKE metacharacters**: `_` is SQL's single-character wildcard, so a subagent named `gke_cluster` was previously matching a sibling named `gkeXcluster`. Closes [#638](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/638).
- config/tools/permissions: plan-first gating gains an **advisory** posture via the new three-valued `permissions.plan_mode` (`off` | `advisory` | `required`) and `--plan-mode`. `required` is what `require_plan_artifact: true` always meant — `record_plan` is registered and the gate denies every mutating call until the plan is on file. `advisory` registers `record_plan` and persists the artifact identically, but **never arms the gate**: the plan becomes an audit trail rather than a precondition. That combination was previously inexpressible, and its absence pushed unattended deployments into a bad trade — arm the gate and have the run stall waiting for an approval nobody is attached to send, or turn plan-first off and lose the recorded reasoning entirely. The `record_plan` **tool description is now mode-aware**: under `required` it tells the model its next mutating call will be denied without a plan, under `advisory` it says the opposite explicitly, and the `/replan` response switches on the same state. Telling a model a gate exists when nothing will enforce it makes it behave as though one does — the inverse of, and the same defect class as, stating a safety property the runtime doesn't enforce. No consumer reads either config field directly: `ResolvedPlanMode()` folds mode-over-bool and the two predicates `PlanToolRegistered()` / `PlanGateArmed()` are what registration and enforcement each call, so the registration and gate decisions cannot drift apart. `permissions.require_plan_artifact` is **deprecated** but still honored (it means `required`); `plan_mode` outranks it, except that `plan_mode: "off"` alongside `require_plan_artifact: true` is rejected at config load rather than silently resolved, since either reading leaves an operator wrong about whether the gate is armed. `--plan-first` remains as a deprecated alias. Precedence, highest first: `--plan-mode`, `--plan-first`, `permissions.plan_mode`, `permissions.require_plan_artifact`, the `--task` profile default; the resolved mode and its source are printed at startup. New `examples/plan-first/.agents/config.advisory.json.example`. Closes [#215](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/215).
- tools: new `wait_and_verify` built-in closes the **verify half of closed-loop remediation**. Fix-and-verify — apply a change, wait for the state to converge, confirm it — had no wait: the only one available was `bash sleep`, which the distroless recipes disable on purpose (the shipped `k8s-triage` skill literally instructed the model to "sleep the verify interval" in a recipe with `tools.disable: ["bash"]`), and the only alternative was re-checking across turns at a full prompt per look. So agents asserted `RESOLVED` instead of observing it ([#639](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/639) through a different door). The tool polls another tool until its result satisfies a condition — `expect_jq` (gojq, same engine as `json_query`), `expect_contains`, `expect_not_contains` — and returns the **whole loop as one tool result**: `verified`, `outcome` (`verified` | `timeout` | `attempts_exhausted` | `canceled`), attempts, elapsed, and a per-attempt observation trail that is the evidence a human can audit. Three properties are enforced rather than prompted: it is pure Go (no shell, works in `distroless/static-debian12:nonroot`); it is bounded on wall clock *and* attempt count with operator ceilings (`tools.wait_and_verify.max_timeout_seconds` / `max_attempts`) the model can't raise — a request past the ceiling is an error, not a silent clamp, since a silently shortened budget is exactly the unenforced-claim pattern this milestone removes — and token cost is bounded by construction, N polls collapsing into one result; and it is **read-only by construction**, refusing to poll anything the runtime can't classify read-only (`IsReadOnlyTool`, [#460](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/460)), because a loop that can call `write_file` sixty times is an amplifier, not a verifier. `wait_and_verify` itself and `ask_user` are refused unconditionally. Since ADK's MCP adapter does not surface the server's `readOnlyHint`, every MCP tool classifies as mutating; the new `tools.wait_and_verify.poll_allow` list is the operator's explicit per-tool assertion that a named MCP tool only observes. Polling adds no authority — each attempt dispatches through the same gate, path/URL scope, plan-first gating and output caps a direct model call hits — and the catalog is late-bound inside `agent.New` (and `RunSubtask`, against the subtask's own tool subset) so an embedder can't forget the wiring. An unverified wait returns normally with `verified: false`: "it never became Ready in three minutes" is a finding to report, not a tool failure to retry. See `docs/wait-and-verify-design.md`. Closes [#648](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/648).
- attach/agent: a **tripped guardrail now survives a process restart**. The behavioral watchdog's enforce-mode halt and the cost ceiling's halt both lived only in the `Agent` struct, so a crash, an OOM kill, or a pod roll started a fresh process with the backstop disarmed — the runaway-loop → crash → restart cycle the [#623](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/623)–[#627](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/627) train exists to break could repeat indefinitely, each restart handing the loop a clean budget, and [#642](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/642) made both backstops default-on for exactly the unattended deployments a supervisor restarts. The trip is now a fact in the eventlog rather than a field in a struct: a halt appends a `guardrail-trip` row (`Author=agent/guardrail-trip`) and an effective operator reset appends `attach-guardrail-reset` (`Author=attach/guardrail-reset`), and the next process over the same session folds those rows forward before its first pre-flight — so a halted session comes back halted, a cleared one comes back cleared, and budget an operator granted before the restart isn't lost to a pod roll it didn't cause. The reset row is the same row as [#331](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/331)'s audit record (two rows meaning "the operator reset this" would be two things to keep in agreement), and it is now written agent-side on a background context rather than inside the HTTP handler — a row written *because* of a caller must not be droppable *by* that caller hanging up, and an out-of-band append would race the runner's in-flight session write (the [#565](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/565) lesson). Restore is lazy and self-installing (once per agent, at the top of the first `Run`) rather than wired per construction site, so every path that builds an agent over an existing session gets the property without remembering to ask; new `Agent.RestoreGuardrails(ctx)` for embedders that want it settled before accepting traffic. Restored state never overrules live configuration — no watchdog halt under `--watchdog=warn`, no cost halt with no ceiling configured, no granted budget applied to a per-session bound that is now disabled — and restore fails **open**, because turning a transient database hiccup into a permanently dead session is worse than the window it covers. Requires an eventlog (`--session-db` / `WithEventLog`); in-memory behavior is unchanged. Closes [#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643).
- attach/tui/agent: a session halted by the behavioral watchdog or a cost ceiling can now be **un-halted by an operator**. Both backstops set a flag that makes the next `Run` refuse at pre-flight, both had in-process reset methods, and neither had a caller — no HTTP endpoint, no slash command, nothing in the CLI — so a halted daemon session was halted permanently and the only recovery was killing the process and losing the conversation you were trying to rescue. The trip messages even named the unexported-to-operators Go methods, advice nobody holding a TUI connection can act on. New `GET /sessions/{id}/guardrails` reports live state (watchdog mode + tripped, both ceilings, session spend, `halted`, and `would_retrip`), and `POST /sessions/{id}/guardrails/reset` clears it, scoped by `guardrail` (`watchdog` | `cost_ceiling` | `all`, default `all`); the local `--local` TUI gets the same through `/guardrail [reset …]`, routed through the same adapter methods so the two surfaces can't drift. Resolving [#331](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/331): a cost reset is **bump-only** (`additional_budget_usd` raises the ceiling) and never rewinds the accumulator — zeroing spend would make `/usage`, `Agent.SessionCostUSD` and the eventlog-derived cost disagree about what the session actually cost. It follows that clearing a *per-session* trip without new budget produces a session that re-halts on the very next turn, so that request is refused with `409 Conflict` naming the shortfall rather than returning a 200 that achieves nothing; the refusal is atomic (nothing mutates), and a *per-turn* trip resets bare since the next turn opens a fresh window. Reset requires `session:write` — not a new admin action, since the caller who can `POST /inject` can already spend the money — and is deliberately not per-caller cost-limited, because rate-limiting the way out of a cost trip is a trap. Every effective reset writes an `attach-guardrail-reset` eventlog row with the caller identity, what was cleared, and the budget added. The `cost_ceiling` capability key, previously hardcoded `false`, now reports whether a bound is actually armed, and a new `guardrails` key reports endpoint serviceability. See `docs/guardrail-reset-design.md`. Closes [#666](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/666) and [#331](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/331).
- config: new `safety.watchdog` (`off` | `warn` | `feedback` | `enforce` — see the `feedback` entry below) makes the behavioral watchdog expressible **in a recipe** instead of only on the command line. Precedence is `--watchdog` > `safety.watchdog` > the mode-dependent default, matching the `--small-tier-parent` / `safety.small_tier_parent` pair it sits next to; an unknown value fails loudly at config load. Before this, a self-contained hardened recipe could set `permissions.require_plan_artifact`, `tools.disable`, cost ceilings, alert targets, and path/url scope — but not its own runaway-loop backstop, so every deploy manifest and every `core-agent -c …` invocation had to remember `--watchdog=enforce` by hand or silently get observe-and-log. The resolved mode now also reaches the agents a multi-session daemon creates through `POST /sessions` (new `compose.SessionFactoryDeps.WatchdogMode`), which previously got no watchdog at all regardless of flags; `Agent.WatchdogMode()` reports the resolved posture, and the startup line names where it came from (`watchdog: enforce mode [unattended default]`). Closes [#660](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/660).
- attach/tui/compose: configured subagents are now **operator-visible** as a first-class roster, distinct from the live-instance view. A new read-only `GET /sessions/{sid}/subagents` returns the roster the daemon loaded — `{name, description, model, root, modes}` per entry — where `modes` is `["sync","async"]` for a declarative subagent the session's own agent carries as a parent tool (see the `#741` entry under **Bug or Regression** — a session created via `POST /sessions` is async-only) or `["async"]` for a predefined spec; it is deliberately separate from `GET .../agents`, which stays "what's running." `GET .../tools` entries now carry a `source` (`builtin`/`mcp`/`skill`/`subagent`/`other`) so a declarative subagent wired as a parent tool is marked as such — sourced from the `Agent` itself, so the classification holds even under `--no-background-agents` (where the async roster is empty because nothing is spawnable by reference). The local `--local` TUI's `/subagent` (no-args) command lists the configured catalog, and the daemon's boot / `--print-config` startup summary gains a `subagents:` line so an operator can verify the loaded roster without grepping logs. No new model-facing tool is added — this is operator-facing only, keeping the pull-tool surface that [#625](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/625) shrank. Closes [#627](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/627).
- config: new top-level `content_roots[]` (plus the repeatable `--agents-content-dir` flag) trusts external directories as additional instruction/skill scopes, so a recipe can run an **unmodified** external agent tree — a checkout of another repo's `agents/…` layout — without vendoring a copy into `.agents/`. Each root is loaded as its own trusted scope: `@include`s resolve *within* the root and escapes are still rejected; instruction scopes concatenate ahead of the project overlay and skills follow first-declarer-wins at precedence project > content_roots (listed order) > home-agents > user. A missing root is a loud error; MCP is not auto-loaded (translate it into the recipe's own `mcp.json`). CLI values merge after the config list and resolve relative to the agents dir. See `docs/external-content-root-design.md`. Part of [#600](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/600).
- tools: new native `alert` built-in tool — a distroless-safe way for a headless daemon to fire escalations to operator-registered webhook targets without a shell or a separate MCP process. Targets are declared in the new `alerts.targets[]` config block (each a named `webhook` with `url`/`url_env`, a `template`, optional per-target `auth` resolved from env, and an LLM-facing `description`); the model fires a target **by name**, so SSRF is impossible by construction — there is no arbitrary-URL parameter. The tool is registered only when at least one target is configured (the `fetch_url` conditional-registration pattern) and is gated per target via `permissions.allow: ["alert:<name>"]`. An optional `alerts.rate_limit_per_target` (`"1/30s"`, `"5/min"`, `"30s"`, …) bounds each target independently to catch runaway escalation loops. Response status + duration are returned; the response body and all auth material stay out of the result and the eventlog. This Phase 1 ships the `generic` JSON-passthrough template; the `slack`/`discord`/`pagerduty_events_v2` templates are designed in `docs/alert-tool-design.md` and rejected at config-load time with a clear "not yet implemented" error until they land. Implements the tool the historical CHANGELOG/[#193](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/193) design promised but never shipped. Part of [#593](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/593).

- background subagents: `spawn_agent` now shows the parent's model the **configured-subagent roster** in its own schema, implementing the discoverability half of `docs/unified-subagent-invocation-design.md` §5. The tool description gains a `name — description` list of every configured subagent (declarative templates and predefined catalog specs alike), and the `agent` field — previously free text — is constrained to an enum of exactly those names whenever ad-hoc spawning is off (with ad-hoc on the enum is dropped, because an ad-hoc spawn legitimately leaves `agent` empty; the list still appears in the description). Before this, a parent could only delegate to a subagent its persona happened to name, which is why the `kube-platform-agent` recipe hardcodes `cluster` plus a symptom catalog in its `AGENTS.md` — that coupling made a reusable persona fleet-specific. The roster is rendered per request rather than at construction, because the spawn tools are built before `SetSubagentTemplates` registers the declarative subagents. No new model-facing tool is added (§5's constraint), and nothing changes for a deployment with no subagents configured. `background.Spec` gains a `Description` field so embedder-supplied predefined specs can be routed to and appear in the `GET /subagents` catalog. Closes [#640](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/640).

- multi-session: sessions created through `POST /sessions` (and lazily resumed ones) now get their **own background-subagent manager** instead of none. Before this, `GET /sessions/{sid}/subagents` came back empty and no `subagent` capability was advertised for a daemon-created session — while `spawn_agent` *was* present in its tool list, because the daemon-bound spawn tools ride along in the shared builtin registry. The delegation surface and the operator catalog disagreed about what the session could do, and the spawns that did happen ran on the daemon's manager: the daemon-wide gate rather than the session's sub-gate, an arbitrary session's `(app, user, sid)` triple as the parent (`AttachParent` is last-writer-wins), one alert channel multiplexed across tenants, and cross-tenant unions from `ListSubagents` / `ListSubagentCatalog`. A per-session manager fixes all four; the daemon-bound spawn tools are stripped from each session's surface and replaced with session-bound ones, and both eviction *and* daemon shutdown close the manager — spawned subagents run under `context.WithoutCancel`, so nothing else terminates them, and a `SIGTERM` now gives a session's subagents the same bounded cancel-and-drain the daemon's own manager has always had instead of dropping them mid-tool-call at process exit. New seam `compose.SessionFactoryDeps.SessionBackground` (with `SessionBackgroundFactory` / `SessionScope` / `SessionSubagents`); the daemon leaves it nil under `--no-background-agents`, which keeps the previous behavior. Per-subagent *synchronous* tools (`agent.WithSubagents`) remain daemon-only — declarative subagents stay reachable by reference through `spawn_agent`. Closes [#637](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/637).

- permissions/tools/config: the "prefer the native tools over the shell" instruction is now **enforced instead of requested**. `grep`'s description already says it is preferred over `bash grep`/`bash rg`/`bash find`, and `bash`'s own description opens by pointing at the structured tools; measured against a Gemini variant in `docs/gemini-tier1-followup-plan.md`, the model picked bash for search **15 times out of 27** anyway, and one observed session opened with three bash-as-grep calls in its first four and then ran 164 turns and $5.41 without producing a diagnosis. A description is advisory and read once at registration — it gives the model no signal at the moment it makes the wrong choice, because a `grep -rn` that matches nothing reads as "no matches", not as "wrong tool", so the model tries another shell variant. The new gate is in-context and immediate, and names the replacement: the refusal explains that the native tool returns structured `{path, line, text}` matches, honors the permission gate and path scope, and applies output caps. Command classification uses the same `mvdan.cc/sh/v3` parser as the existing safe-command check, so it sees through `;`, `&&`, `||`, subshells and blocks (`make build && grep -rn TODO .` refuses on the right-hand side) — but it deliberately leaves two shapes alone, because the native tools genuinely cannot replace them: a search reading from a **pipe** is a stream filter, and `find` carrying an **action predicate** is a file operation (that predicate set is `verbAutoAllowDenyTokens["find"]`, the list `pkg/permissions/safecmd.go` already maintains for the same reason). This is steering, not security: it fails **open** on anything it can't resolve literally (`$TOOL -rn foo`, `eval`, `sh -c '…'`), since a false refusal on a real build command costs more than a missed nudge — the boundary for bash remains the permission mode and `tools.disable`. Four properties follow the "make the claim true by construction" line the rest of this milestone takes. The check runs **before** the permission mode, so `--yolo` doesn't wave it through. It is **inherited** by every sub-gate a multi-session daemon derives, rather than re-defaulted per session — which would have left the gate off for daemon-created sessions while the boot line still claimed `enforce`. It refuses only what it can **redirect**: `tools.Build` tells the gate which native tools it actually registered, so a recipe that puts `grep` in `tools.disable` and keeps `bash` gets no refusal naming a tool the model can't call (the same unenforceable-claim failure, pointed the other way), and with neither `grep` nor `glob` registered the gate reports itself inert rather than armed. And the posture is **reported on every boot**, including the default, with the binary list read back off the gate — so the line can't advertise a bigger gate than the one in force, and `bash`'s own model-facing description flips from "prefer the structured tools" to "REFUSED" exactly when the refusal is real. Silent when `bash` isn't registered at all. New `safety.bash_search_gate` (`enforce` | `warn` | `allow`, default `enforce`) and `--bash-search-gate`, which overrides it. Closes [#158](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/158).
- taskclass/tools/permissions: a task class is now a **capability** profile, not just a decoding profile. `taskclass.Profile` gains `DisableTools` and `RequirePlanArtifact`, and the three investigation-shaped classes — `debug`, `research`, `review` — use both: they drop `bash` and turn plan-first gating on. The measurement behind it is the same one that motivated [#158](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/158) (bash-as-grep chosen 15 times out of 27, one session burning 164 turns and $5.41 without a diagnosis), taken one step further: the search gate redirects the *search-shaped* subset of bash, while a class that names itself an investigation has no business holding a shell at all. The two compose — with `bash` gone the gate reports itself silent rather than armed, since there is nothing left to refuse. Plan-first is likewise a claim the class makes true rather than a prompt line: mutating tools (including MCP tools, `fetch_url` and `spawn_agent`) are denied until the model records a plan, so "investigate before you change things" is enforced by the dispatch path. Three precedence rules, each with a reason. `--enable-tools=<list>` cancels the **profile's** drop only, never `tools.disable` or `--disable-tools` — a config file that hardens a deployment is a claim the runtime keeps, so naming a tool both places is a startup error that says where the other half lives, and an unknown tool name is a startup error at all times rather than a silent no-op that depends on whether `--task` happened to be set. `--plan-first[=false]` beats the config field, which beats the profile; a profile can only turn the gate **on**, never off, because a class saying "no plan needed" would silently undo an operator's `require_plan_artifact: true`. And the profile's plan-first is **suppressed with an explanatory line** — not silently applied — when `record_plan` can't register (`--no-builtin-tools`, no resolved `.agents/` directory, or `record_plan` in either disable list), because `/replan` only revokes a plan and never grants one: the gate would deny every mutating call for the life of the session with nothing able to clear it. Explicit operator intent in that same situation is honored, with a loud warning naming the specific blocker. Every start prints the resolved posture (`tools=default−bash`, `plan-first: on (task profile)`). Closes [#160](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/160).

- watchdog/agent/config: the runaway-behavior watchdog now has a **`feedback` mode that tells the model what it is doing**, and `enforce` includes it. The watchdog's one shipped signal fires when the agent makes five byte-identical tool calls in a row — and every existing mode routed that observation to a reader who cannot act on it: `warn` logs a line for an operator who, in the unattended runs [#642](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/642) just defaulted to a backstop, is a pod log nobody is tailing; `enforce` halts the process. The one party in a position to *stop looping* — the model choosing the next tool call — was never told. The modes are now a ladder (`off` < `warn` < `feedback` < `enforce`), each including the one before it, and options are appended additively at both wiring sites (`cmd/core-agent`, `compose.SessionFactoryDeps`) so a stronger rung can't drift from the weaker one it contains. Under `feedback` and above, the next turn's prompt is prefixed with a `[watchdog]` block naming the signal and what to do differently, ahead of the pending-alert and inbox prepends so it reads first. `enforce` implying feedback is the load-bearing half: an enforce halt is cleared by an operator reset ([#666](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/666)), and that reset resumes a model whose context still ends in the loop it was halted for, so without the injection the reset is a treadmill — the same five calls, the same halt, one operator round-trip later. `ResetWatchdog` therefore clears the halt but deliberately keeps the queued observation, and a halt restored from the eventlog after a restart ([#643](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/643)) synthesizes the observation from the persisted reason, since the in-memory queue died with the process and durable-halt-without-durable-correction is half a property. `watchdog.Alert` splits into an operator-facing `Reason` (may name `/interrupt`, `--max-turn-cost-usd`) and a model-facing `Guidance` that names no operator affordance the model doesn't have — a correction that tells the model to "consider `/interrupt`" is noise at best; `FormatFeedback` falls back to `Reason` so a third-party `Signal` that sets no `Guidance` still produces feedback rather than being silently inert. `warn` is deliberately unchanged, because altering it would silently rewrite the context of every operator already running it. The queue is bounded (4 alerts, oldest dropped), nothing is queued while the mode is below `feedback` so a later flip can't deliver a stale backlog, and the block is framed as an automated observation the user cannot see — it is not a trust boundary (a prompt can contain the literal string, as with `[Inbox]`). This is a correction, not a backstop: nothing halts a model that reads the block and loops anyway, so unattended runs still want `enforce` — which now gets both. Closes [#159](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/159).

- watchdog: a **second loop detector**, plus argument canonicalization — the two evasions the v1 detector named in its own docstring and could not catch. `repeated-tool-call` requires a *consecutive* run of one call, so wedging any second call into the loop hid it; the live GKE UAT loop that survived an operator `stop` *and* `/interrupt` ([#622](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/pull/622)) was exactly that shape, `list_agents → check_agent` forever. New `alternating-tool-cycle` scans the recent call history for a 2–4 call sequence repeated three times with byte-identical arguments each lap, and is wired into `NewDefaultWatchdog` alongside the repeat detector — both `Critical`, so both halt under `--watchdog=enforce` and both reach the model under `--watchdog=feedback` ([#159](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/159)). Arguments are now compared path-canonicalized rather than as literal strings: values under path-shaped keys are cleaned, so `main.go`, `./main.go` and `dir/../main.go` are one call, and the consecutive detector additionally folds a genuine component-boundary suffix (`/workspace/main.go` ≡ `main.go`) — an agent re-reading one file under three spellings is as stuck as one re-reading it under the same spelling. The generalization is deliberately narrow in both directions, because a Critical alert halts the agent: basename matching is rejected (`a/doc.go` vs `b/doc.go` would false-positive on every repo with repeated filenames), non-path values are never normalized even when they look like paths (a `grep` pattern, a `bash` command), two laps is not a cycle (read-grep-read-grep is ordinary exploration), and a block of one repeated call is left to the repeat detector so one behavior never raises two alerts. Cycle alerts are fingerprinted rotation-invariantly, so a loop that presents as `a → b` on one call and `b → a` on the next still gets one notice rather than one per call. The known false positive — a hand-rolled polling loop written as alternating calls — is what `wait_and_verify` ([#648](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/648)) exists to replace, and an embedder can construct `DefaultWatchdog` with their own signal list. Semantic ("same question, different phrasing") detection stays v3.0 scope. Closes [#649](https://github.com/go-steer/core-agent/issues/649).

#### Bug or Regression
- pricing/tui/taskclass/usage: **the model tables are now one table with three narrowings, instead of four hand-maintained lists that had drifted apart.** `pkg/pricing`'s builtin rates, the `/model` picker, the task-class tier defaults and `usage`'s context-window map each enumerated models independently, and each had aged at its own rate. The picker was the visible casualty: six of its nineteen entries (`gemini-2.5-pro`, `gemini-2.5-flash`, `gemini-3-flash-preview`, `gemini-3.1-pro-preview`, `gemini-3.1-pro-preview-customtools`, `gemini-3.1-flash-image-preview`) resolved to no rate at all, which shows as `$—` in the TUI and, far worse, makes `--max-turn-cost-usd` / `--max-session-cost-usd` **silently inert** for anyone who picked one — an unpriced turn costs 0, and a ceiling on 0 never trips. The fix is structural rather than six new rows. The generator's hand-curated allowlist is replaced by a **rule**: every model in LiteLLM's catalog whose id starts `gemini-`/`claude-`, whose `mode` is `chat`, that declares `supports_function_calling`, that is not past its `deprecation_date`, and that carries both a rate and a `max_input_tokens` — 12 entries become 31, and a model added upstream on a Monday needs no second edit here. `supports_function_calling` is decoded as `*bool` because an absent flag in that catalog does not mean `false`, and every near-miss is printed with the reason it was dropped, so the weekly PR shows what the rule *excluded* and not just what it kept.
